TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY
FINITE TREES
OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Abstract. We investigate the interrelations between the metric prope rties, or-
der properties and combinatorial properties of the set of ba lls in totally bounded
ultrametric space. In particular, the Gurvich—Vyalyi repre sentation of ﬁnite, ul-
trametric spaces by monotone rooted trees is generalized to the case of totally
bounded ultrametric spaces. It is shown that such spaces hav e isometric comple-
tions if and only if their labeled representing trees are iso morphic. We characterize
up to isomorphism the representing trees of these spaces and , up to order isomor-
phism, the posets of open balls in such spaces.

1. Introduction
In 2001 at the Workshop on General Algebra the attention of ex perts on the
theory of lattices was paid to the following problem of I. M. G elfand: Using graph
theory describe up to isometry all ﬁnite ultrametric spaces [85]. An appropriate
representation of ﬁnite, ultrametric spaces by monotone ro oted trees was proposed
by V. Gurvich and M. Vyalyi in [63]. A simple geometric descri ption of Gurvich—
Vyalyi representing trees was found in [97]. This descripti on allows us eﬀectively
use the Gurvich—Vyalyi representation in various problems associated with ﬁnite
ultrametric spaces. In particular, this leads to a graph-th eoretic interpretation of
the Gomory—Hu inequality [51]. A characterization of ﬁnite ultrametric spaces
which are as rigid as possible also was obtained [52] on the ba sis of the Gurvich—
Vyalyi representation. Some other extremal properties of ﬁ nite ultrametric spaces
and related them properties of monotone rooted trees have be en found in [50]. The
interconnections between the Gurvich—Vyalyi representat ion and the space of balls
endowed with the Hausdorﬀ metric are discussed in [33] (see a lso [48,95,98,105,106]).
It is well-known that the sets of leaves of phylogenetic equi distant trees with
additive metric are ultrametric. The ﬁnite equidistant tre es can be considered as
ﬁnite subtrees of the so-called R-trees (see [8] for some interesting results related
toR-trees and ultrametrics). A description of interrelations between ﬁnite subtrees
of R-trees and ﬁnite, monotone rooted trees can be found in [36]. The categorical
equivalence of trees and ultrametric spaces was investigat ed in [71] and [86].
The paper is organized as follows.
In Sections 2 and 3 we collect together some properties of ult rametric balls and,
respectively, of totally bounded metric spaces.
In Proposition 4.1 of Section 4.1 we consider a generalizati on of strong triangle in-
equality for arbitrary cycle of points of ultrametric space and give some applications
of this generalization in Propositions 4.4 and 4.5.
New interrelations between complete multipartite graphs a nd diametrical graphs
of ultrametric spaces are found in Section 4.2. In particula r, using complete multi-
partite graphs we found new necessary and suﬃcient conditio ns under which metric
spaces are ultrametric, Theorem 4.17. In Theorems 5.29 and 5 .41 of Section 5.3
we characterize bounded ultrametric spaces which are weakl y similar to unbounded
ones via diametrical graphs. Characteristic properties of functions preserving totally
bounded ultrametrics are proved in Theorem 5.59 of Section 5 .3.
The distance set of totally bounded ultrametric spaces and s eparable metric spaces
are characterized in Theorem 5.19 and Proposition 5.23 of Se ction 5.2.
In section 6, we prove that the ballean (the set of all open bal ls) of totally bounded
ultrametric space is a subset of all closed balls of this spac e, Corollary 6.2, and ﬁnd
conditions under which the converse inclusion holds, Corol lary 6.8. Proposition 6.12
and Theorem 6.13 of the section show that the balleans of any t wo dense subsets of
arbitrary ultrametric space are order isomorphic with resp ect to the partial order
generated by set inclusion.
The balleans endowed with the Hausdorﬀ metric are described in Theorem 7.9 of
Section 7 for compact, ultrametric space. Using this theore m we show that every
totally bounded ultrametric space admits an isometric embe dding in a compact,
ultrametric space with the dense set of isolated points, The orem 7.11.

The new results related to the representing trees of ultrame tric spaces are mainly
concentrated in Sections 8, 9 and 10. In particular, in Secti on 10 we consider the
labeled trees which represent the totally bounded ultramet ric spaces that general-
izes the Gurvich—Vyalyi representation of ﬁnite ultrametr ic spaces. A constructive
characterization of such trees is given in Theorem 11.11. Mo reover, the correspond-
ing characterizations for rooted trees without labeling an d for free trees are given in
Proposition 9.18 and Theorem 11.16, respectively.
Theorem 9.9 from Section 9 presents also a description of par tial orders generated
by choice of a root in arbitrary tree. That allows us to charac terize in Theorem 11.17
all posets which are order isomorphic to posets of the open ba lls from totally bounded
ultrametric spaces.
2. Ultrametric balls
In the paper the ultrametric balls are used as a starting poin t in the construction
of representing trees corresponding to totally bounded ult rametric spaces. The main
goal of this section is to recall some known properties of bal ls in ultrametric spaces.
Let us start from basic concepts.
A metric on a set X is a function d : X × X → R+ such that for all x, y, z ∈ X
(i) d(x,y ) = d(y,x ),
(ii) (d(x,y ) = 0) ⇔ (x =y),
(iii) d(x,y ) ≤ d(x,z ) +d(z,y ).
A metric space (X,d ) is ultrametric if the strong triangle inequality
d(x,y ) ≤ max{d(x,z ),d (z,y )}.
holds for all x, y, z ∈ X. In this case the function d is called an ultrametric on X.
Deﬁnition 2.1. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be metric spaces. A mapping Φ :X → Y is
called to be an isometric embedding of (X,d ) in (Y,ρ ) if
d(x,y ) = ρ(Φ(x), Φ(y))
holds for all x, y ∈ X. In the case when Φ is bijective, we say that it is an isometry
of (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ). The metric spaces are isometric if there is an isometry of these
spaces.
Let A be a subset of a metric space (X,d ), A ⊆ X. The quantity
(2.1) diamA = diam(A,d ) = sup {d(x,y ) :x,y ∈ A}
is the diameter of A. If the inequality diamA <∞ holds, then we say that A is a
bounded subset of X.
We deﬁne the distance set D(X) of a metric space (X,d ) as
D(X) = D(X,d ) = {d(x,y ) :x,y ∈ X}.
It is clear that diamX = supD(X).
If (X,d ) is an ultrametric space and A is a nonempty subset of X, then, using
the strong triangle inequality, we can easily prove the equa lity
(2.2) diamA = sup{d(x,a ) :x ∈ A}
for every a ∈ A [33].
PAGE 4
4 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Let (X,d ) be a metric space. An open ball with a radiusr >0 and a centerc ∈ X
is the set
Br(c) = {x ∈ X : d(c,x )<r }.
Let c be a point of a metric space (X,d ) and let r ⩾ 0. Similarly to open balls
we deﬁne the closed ball Br(c) with a radius r and a center c as
(2.3) Br(c) = {x ∈ X : d(c,x ) ⩽r}.
Thus, the equality Br(c) = {c} holds if r = 0.
Now we are ready to introduce the ﬁrst, basic for us, deﬁnitio n.
Deﬁnition 2.2. The ballean of a metric space (X,d ) is the set BX = B(X,d) of all
open balls in (X,d ).
In what follows, we will also denote by BX = B(X,d) the set of all closed balls in
(X,d ). An arbitrary B ∈ BX ∪ BX is a ball in (X,d ).
Remark 2.3. The term ballean, which was introduced in Deﬁnition 2.2, is a lso used
to mean a set X endowed with a special family of subsets of X × X. See, for example,
[102–104].
Remark 2.4. Usually, when deﬁning closed balls, only strictly positive values of the
radiusr are used (see, for example, Deﬁnition 5.1.1 in [115]). Break ing this tradition
in present paper can be partially justiﬁed by following tech nical advantages:
(i) Every chain of closed balls in spherically complete ultrame tric space has
a meet and, in particular, every maximal chain of such balls c ontains the
smallest ball.
(ii) For each metric space (X,d ), the mapping
X ∋ x ↦→ { x} ∈
BX
is an isometric embedding of (X,d ) in BX if we endowed BX with the
Hausdorﬀ metric.
Let (X,d ) be a metric space. A subset A of X is open if for every a ∈ A there
is r >0 such that Br(a) ⊆ A. A set C ⊆ X is said to be closed if its complement
X \C is open. Moreover, we say that a set Y ⊆ X is clopen if it is closed and open
simultaneously.
For every metric space (X,d ), the closed balls are closed subsets of X and the
open balls are open subsets of X. A much stronger result is true if (X,d ) is an
ultrametric space.
Recall that a point p in a metric space (X,d ) is isolated if there is ε> 0 such that
d(p,x )>ε for every x ∈ X \ {p}. If p is not an isolated point of X, then p is called
an accumulation point of X.
Proposition 2.5. Let (X,d ) be ultrametric. Then the following statements hold:
(i) For every r ∈ (0, ∞ ) and every c ∈ X the balls Br(c) and Br(c) are clopen
subsets of X.
(ii) If r = 0 , then the ball Br(c) is clopen if and only if c is an isolated point
of X.
Proof. Statement (i) follows directly from Proposition 18.4 [113]. Statement (ii) is
easy to prove for arbitrary metric space. □

The following proposition claims that every point of an arbi trary ultrametric ball
is a center of that ball.
Proposition 2.6. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space. Then for every ball Br(c)
(
Br(c)) and every a ∈ Br(c) (a ∈ Br(c)) we have
Br(c) = Br(a) ( Br(c) = Br(a)).
Proof. It is a simple modiﬁcation of Proposition 18.4 [113]. □
Using Proposition 2.6 it is easy to prove that if B is an open ball in ultrametric
space (X,d ) and if B1 is an open ball in (B,d |B× B), then B1 is also an open ball in
(X,d ).
Corollary 2.7. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space. Then the inclusion
(2.4) BB ⊆ BX
holds for every B ∈ BX
Remark 2.8. It is interesting to note that Proposition 2.6 gives us a char acteristic
property of the ultrametric spaces (see Proposition 1.7 [33 ]). On the other hand,
even if inclusion (2.4) holds for every B ∈ BX, then, in general, it does not imply the
ultrametricity of (X,d ). Indeed, if X = R and d is the standard Euclidean metric
on R, then we evidently have (2.4) for every B ∈ BX, but (X,d ) is not ultrametric.
Lemma 2.9. LetBr(c) be a closed ball in an ultrametric space (X,d ) and let
r1 = diamBr(c).
Then the equality Br1(c) = Br(c) holds.
Proof. Using the deﬁnition of Br(c) and equality (2.2) with A = Br(c) and a = c,
we obtain the inequality r1 ⩽r. Consequently, Br1(c) ⊆ Br(c) holds. To prove the
converse inclusion it suﬃces to note that d(x,c ) ⩽ diamBr(c) = r1 holds for every
x ∈ Br(c), that implies Br(c) ⊆ Br1(c). □
Lemma 2.9 and Proposition 2.6 give us the next statement.
Proposition 2.10. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space and let B1, B2 ∈ BX. Then
B1 ⊆ B2 holds if and only if B1 ∩ B2 ̸= ∅ and
diamB1 ⩽ diamB2.
Remark 2.11. In Proposition 2.10 we cannot replace the condition
• B1, B2 ∈ BX
for the condition
• B1, B2 ∈ BX
as the following example shows.
Example 2.12. Let X be the intersection of the set Q of all rational numbers with
the interval [0, 1] of the real line R,X = {x ∈ Q : 0 ⩽x ⩽ 1}. Following Delhommé,
Laﬂamme, Pouzet and Sauer [28], we deﬁne an ultrametric d : X × X → R+ as
(2.5) d(x,y ) =

Br1(0) = Q ∩ [0, 1) ̸=Br2(0) = Q ∩ [0, 1].
The next proposition is a modiﬁcation of Proposition 18.5 [1 13].
Proposition 2.13. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space and let B1 andB2 be diﬀerent
balls in (X,d ). Then we have either B1 ⊂ B2 or B2 ⊂ B1 whenever B1 ∩ B2 ̸= ∅.
If B1 and B2 are disjoint, B1 ∩ B2 = ∅, then the equality
(2.6) d(x1,x 2) = diam(B1 ∪ B2)
holds for every x1 ∈ B1 and every x2 ∈ B2.
Proof. By Proposition 18.5 [113], we have
(B1 ⊂ B2 or B2 ⊂ B1) ⇔ (B1 ∩ B2 ̸= ∅)
and, moreover, the same proposition claims the equality
(2.7) d(x1,x 2) = d(y1,y 2)
for all x1, y1 ∈ B1 and all x2, y2 ∈ B2 wheneverB1 ∩ B2 = ∅.
Write
(2.8) ∆(B1,B 2) = sup {d(x1,x 2) :x1 ∈ B1 and x2 ∈ B2}.
It follows directly from (2.1) that
(2.9) diam(B1 ∪ B2) = max
{
diamB1, diamB2, ∆(B1,B 2)
}
.
Now, using (2.7) and (2.9) we see that (2.6) holds if
(2.10) diamBi ⩽ ∆(B1,B 2), i = 1, 2.
Without loss of generality, we may assume diamB1 ⩾ diamB2. Now, if (2.10) does
not hold, then there are z1, y1 ∈ B1 such that
(2.11) d(z1,y 1)> ∆(B1,B 2).
Let r1 be the radius of B1. Using Proposition 2.6 we can consider z1 as a center of
the ball B1. By inequality (2.11),
r1 ⩾d(z1,y 1)>d (z1,x 2)
holds for every x2 ∈ B2. Thus, B2 ⊆ B1, contrary to B1 ∩ B2 = ∅. □
Remark 2.14. Let (X,d ) be a metric space. For any nonempty subsets A and B of
X, the distance betweenA and B is deﬁned as
(2.12) dist(A,B ) = inf {d(x,y ) :x ∈ A,y ∈ B}.
It was shown in above mentioned Proposition 18.5 [113] that d(x1,x 2) = dist(B1,B 2)
holds for all x1 ∈ B1 and x2 ∈ B2 if B1 and B2 are disjoint balls and d is an
ultrametric. Thus, from (2.6) it follows that
(2.13) dist(B1,B 2) = diam(B1 ∪ B2) = ∆(B1,B 2)
wheneverB1 andB2 are disjoint, ultrametric balls and ∆(B1,B 2) is deﬁned by (2.8).
Remark 2.15. A partial generalization of Propositions 2.6, 2.10 and 2.13 , for “balls”
whose points are ﬁnite, nonempty subsets of X, is given in Proposition 2.1 [32].

Deﬁnition 2.16. Let (X,d ) be a metric space and let A be a nonempty, bounded
subset of X. A ball B∗ =B∗(A) ∈ BX is the smallest ball containing A if B∗ ⊇ A
and the implication
(2.14) (B ⊇ A) ⇒ (B ⊇ B∗)
is valid for every B ∈
BX .
It is clear that, for every nonempty, bounded A ⊆ X, the smallest ball B∗(A) is
unique if it exists. The next proposition implies, in partic ular, the existence of such
balls in every nonempty, ultrametric space (X,d ).
Proposition 2.17 ([33]). Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space, let A be a bounded
subset of X and let a ∈ A. Then the equality B∗(A) = Br(a) holds with r = diamA.
The following corollary of Proposition 2.17 shows that cond ition (2.14) is equiva-
lent to some weaker condition, if (X,d ) is ultrametric.
Corollary 2.18 ([33]). Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space, B1 ∈ BX and let A be a
nonempty bounded subset of B1. Then B1 =B∗(A) holds if and only if
(B ⊇ A) ⇒ (diamB ⩾ diamB1)
is valid for every B ∈ BX .
Proposition 2.17 can be also reformulated as follows.
Corollary 2.19. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space, let A be a nonempty bounded
subset of X and let B1 ∈ BX . Then B1 = B∗(A) holds if and only if A ∩ B1 ̸= ∅
and diamB1 = diamA.
3. Totally bounded metric spaces
The object of our studies is totally bounded ultrametric spa ces. In the present
section we mainly collect together some known properties of totally bounded metric
spaces.
An important subclass of totally bounded metric spaces is th e class of compact
metric spaces.
Deﬁnition 3.1 (Borel—Lebesgue property) . Let (X,d ) be a metric space. A set A
of X is compact if every family F ⊆ BX satisfying the inclusion
A ⊆
⋃
B∈F
B
contains a ﬁnite subfamily F0 ⊆ F , |F0|< ∞ , such that
A ⊆
⋃
B∈F 0
B.
A standard deﬁnition of compactness usually formulated as: Every open cover of
A in X has a ﬁnite subcover.
There exists a simple interdependence between the compactn ess of a set A ⊆ X
and the so-called convergent sequences in A. A sequence (xn)n∈ N of points in a
metric space (X,d ) is said to converge to a point a ∈ X,
(3.1) lim
n→∞
xn =a,
PAGE 8
8 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
if, for every open ball B ∋ a, it is possible to ﬁnd an integer n0 ⩾ 1 such thatxn ∈ B
for every n ⩾n0. Thus, (3.1) holds if and only if
lim
n→∞
d(xn,a ) = 0.
The following classical theorem was proved by Frechet.
Proposition 3.2 (Bolzano—Weierstrass property) . A subset A of a metric space is
compact if and only if every inﬁnite sequence of points of A contains a subsequence
which converges to a point of A.
This result and a long list of distinct criteria of compactne ss can be found, for
example, in [115, p. 206].
It is clear that a subset A of a metric space (X,d ) is bounded if there is a ball B
in (X,d ) such that A ⊆ B.
Now we present a deﬁnition of total boundedness in the spirit of Borel—Lebesgue
property.
Deﬁnition 3.3. A subset A of a metric space (X,d ) is totally bounded if for every
r> 0 there is a ﬁnite set {Br(x1),...,B r(xn)} ⊆ BX such that
A ⊆
n⋃
i=1
Br(xi).
It is easy to see that every totally bounded set is bounded and every compact set
is totally bounded.
To formulate an analog of Bolzano—Weierstrass property for totally bounded
metric spaces we recall that, for metric space (X,d ), a sequence (xn)n∈ N ⊆ X is a
Cauchy sequence in (X,d ) if, for every r > 0, there is an integer n0 = n0(r) ⩾ 1
such that xn ∈ Br(xn0) for every n ⩾n0. It is easy to see that (xn)n∈ N is a Cauchy
sequence if and only if
limm→∞
n→∞
d(xn,x m) = 0
holds.
Remark 3.4. Here and later for a set X and sequence (xn)n∈ N we write (xn)n∈ N ⊆ X
if and only if xn ∈ X holds for every n ∈ N.
Proposition 3.5. A subset A of a metric space (X,d ) is totally bounded if and only
if every inﬁnite sequence of points of A contains a Cauchy subsequence.
See, for example, Theorem 7.8.2 [115].
Corollary 3.6. If A is a totally bounded subset of (X,d ) and C is a subset of A,
then C is also totally bounded.
Let (X,d ) be a metric space and let A ⊆ X. A subset S of A is said to be dense
in A if for every a ∈ A there is a sequence (sn)n∈ N ⊆ S such that
a = lim
n→∞
sn.
By deﬁnition, a metric space (X,d ) is separable ifX contains an at most countable,
dense (in X) subset. It is clear that every totally bounded metric space is separable.
Deﬁnition 3.7. A metric space (X,d ) is complete if every Cauchy sequence in
(X,d ) converges to a point of X.

It can be proved that for a given metric space (X,d ) there exists exactly one (up
to isometry) complete metric space ( ˜X, ˜d) such that ˜X contains a dense, isometric
to (X,d ), subset (see, for example, Theorem 4.3.19 [57]). The metric space ( ˜X, ˜d)
satisfying the above condition is called the completion of (X,d ).
Proposition 3.8. The completion ( ˜X, ˜d) of a metric space (X,d ) is compact if and
only if (X,d ) is totally bounded.
For the proof see, for example, Corollary 4.3.30 in [57].
Lemma 3.9. The completion ( ˜X, ˜d) is ultrametric for every ultrametric (X,d ).
Proof. Since ˜d is a metric on ˜X, to prove that ( ˜X, ˜d) is ultrametric it suﬃces to

(X ′, ˜d|X ′× X ′) which is isometric to (X,d ). For simplicity we may assume X = X ′
and ˜d|X ′× X ′ =d.

The following result is a modiﬁcation of Lemma 3.19 from [13] .
Proposition 3.10. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be compact metric spaces and let Φ :X →
Y be an isometric embedding. If there is an isometric embeddin g Ψ :Y → X, then
Φ is an isometry.

The following lemma will be useful for description of distan ce sets of totally
bounded ultrametric spaces.
Lemma 3.11. For every compact metric space (X,d ) the distance set D(X) is a
compact subset of (R+,ρ ) with respect to the standard Euclidean metric ρ.
Proof. Let (X,d ) be compact. The metric d : X × X → R+ is a continuous function
on the Cartesian product X × X. Any Cartesian product of compact spaces is com-
pact by Tychonoﬀ theorem ([57, Theorem 3.2.4]). Furthermor e, every continuous
image of compact Hausdorﬀ space is compact ([57, Theorem 3.1 .10]). To complete
the proof it suﬃces to note that (X,d ) evidently is a Hausdorﬀ space and that
Cartesian products of Hausdorﬀ spaces are Hausdorﬀ ([57, Th eorem 2.3.11]). □
The following corollary of Lemma 3.11 is a reformulation of T heorem 68 from [78].
Corollary 3.12. For every nonempty, compact metric space (X,d ) there are points
x1, x2 ∈ X such that d(x1,x 2) = diam X.
We conclude the section with an important example of compact ultrametric space.

note that the ring Z of all usual integer numbers is a dense subset of Z2.
Remark 3.15. Hensel’s ring Z2 is not the only known model for Dℵ 0. Among the
many spaces homeomorphic to Dℵ 0, the most popular and well-studied is apparently
the Cantor ternary set. It is a perfect subset of R which was deﬁned in Cantor’s paper
[25]. The interesting information related to Cantor’s set a nd Cantor’s function can
be found in [125] and [45]. Section 8 of the present paper cont ains a new description
of all homeomorphic to Dℵ 0, ultrametric spaces on the language of representing trees
(see Proposition 8.10).

4. To ultrametricity via cycles and complete multipartite gra phs
At the beginning of this section, we recall the concepts of a ( simple) graph and
cycle. A characterization of cycles of points of ultrametri c space is given in Propo-
sition 4.1. The ﬁrst part of the section also contains severa l applications of this
proposition in the proofs of properties of ultrametric spac es.
Theorem 4.17 from the second part of the section gives us a cha racterization of
ultrametric spaces with the help of complete multipartite g raphs. In Theorem 4.10,
it is shown that the diametrical graph of ultrametric space i s nonempty if and only
if it is a complete multipartite graph. Using this result we p rove that, for every
totally bonded ultrametric space, the diametrical graph is complete k-partite with
some k ∈ N (see Proposition 4.11).
4.1. Cycles in ultrametric spaces
A simple graph is a pair (V,E ) consisting of a nonempty set V and a set E
whose elements are unordered pairs {u,v } of diﬀerent points u,v ∈ V . For a graph
G = (V,E ), the sets V = V (G) and E = E(G) are called the set of vertices (or
nodes) and the set of edges , respectively. We say that G is empty if E(G) = ∅. A
graph G is ﬁnite if V (G) is a ﬁnite set, |V (G)|< ∞ . If {x,y } ∈ E(G), then the
verticesx and y are adjacent, and we say that x and y are ends of {x,y }.
A ﬁnite graph C is a cycle if |V (C)| ≥ 3 and there exists an enumeration v1, v2,
... , vn of its vertices such that
({vi,v j} ∈ E(C)) ⇔ (|i − j|= 1 or |i − j|=n − 1).
Using the concept of cycle we can reformulate the condition o f ultrametricity as
follows.
Proposition 4.1. Let (X,d ) be a metric space. Then the following two conditions
are equivalent:
(i) (X,d ) is ultrametric.
(ii) For every cycle C with V (C) ⊆ X there exist at least two diﬀerent edges
{x1,y 1}, {x2,y 2} ∈ E(C) such that
(4.1) d(x1,y 1) = d(x2,y 2) = max {d(x,y ) : {x,y } ∈ E(C)}.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Let |E(C)| be the number of edges in a cycle C, V (C) ⊆ X.
If |E(C)| = 3 , then (4.1) follows from the strong triangle inequality. As sume that
(4.1) is true for |E(C)|⩽n, but that there exists a cycle with |E(C)|=n + 1 which
contains exactly one edge {x,y } such that
d(x,y ) = max {d(u,v ) : {u,v } ∈ E(C)}.
Let z be the vertex of cycle C which is adjacent to y but distinct from x. In view
of the uniqueness of the edge of maximum length, we have
d(y,z )<d (x,y ).
Hence, because of the strong triangle inequality, this impl ies d(x,z ) = d(x,y ). Let
C1 be the cycle for which
V (C1) = V (C) \ {y}, E (C1) =
(
E(C) \
{
{x,y }, {y,z }
})
∪ {x,z }.
Then |E(C1)|=n and {x,z } is the unique edge of maximum length, which contra-
dicts the inductive hypothesis.
PAGE 13
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 13
(ii) ⇒ (i). For the proof of validity of (ii) ⇒ (i) we note that (ii) implies the
strong triangle inequality if |V (C)|= 3. □
Remark 4.2. Proposition 4.1 is, in fact, a simple modiﬁcation of Lemma 1 f rom [46].
Let us consider some useful corollaries of Proposition 4.1. The following result
can be found in [26] (see formula (12), Theorem 1.6).
Corollary 4.3. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty ultrametric space and let A be a dense
subset of X. Then the distance sets of (X,d ) and of (A,d |A× A) are the same,
(4.2) D(A) = D(X).
Proof. It is clear that D(A) ⊆ D(X) and 0 ∈ D(A). Consequently, (4.2) holds if
and only if for each pair of distinct x1, x2 ∈ X there are a1, a2 ∈ A satisfying
(4.3) d(x1,x 2) = d(a1,a 2).
Since x1 ̸=x2 and A is dense in X, we can ﬁnd a1, a2 ∈ A such that
(4.4) d(x1,x 2)>d (xi,a i), i = 1, 2.
Ifx1 =a1 orx2 =a2, then (4.3) follows from the strong triangle inequality. Su ppose
that x1 ̸= a1 and x2 ̸= a2. Using (4.4) we can easily prove that the points a1, x1,
x2, a2 are pairwise distinct. Let us consider the cycle C with
V (C) = {a1,x 1,x 2,a 2} and E(C) =
{
{a1,x 1}, {x1,x 2}, {x2,a 2}, {a2,a 1}
}
.
Equality (4.3) follows from (4.4) by Proposition 4.1. □
Corollary 4.3 and Lemma 3.9 imply the following well-known r esult: “No new
values of the ultrametric after completion” (see, for examp le, [94, p. 4] or [26, The-
orem 1.6, Statement (12)]).
Proposition 4.4. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space and let ( ˜X, ˜d) be the completion
of (X,d ). Then the equality
(4.5) D(X) = D( ˜X)
holds.
In the next known proposition (see, for example, [94, p. 4] or [26, Theorem 1.6,
Statement (13)]) we consider a comfortable “ultrametric mo diﬁcation” of the notion
of Cauchy sequence.
Proposition 4.5. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space. A sequence (xn)n∈ N ⊆ X is a
Cauchy sequence if and only if the limit relation

holds.
Proof. Let (xn)n∈ N ⊆ X be a Cauchy sequence. Then (4.6) follows directly from the
deﬁnition.

(4.7) d(xn,x n+1) ⩽r

d(xn,x n+k) ⩽r

4.2. Complete multipartite graphs and ultrametric spaces
The notion of complete multipartite graph G is well-known when the vertex set
V (G) is ﬁnite. Below we need this concept for graphs having the ver tex sets of
arbitrary cardinality.
Deﬁnition 4.7. Let G be a graph and let k ⩾ 2 be a cardinal number. The graph
G is complete k-partite if the vertex set V (G) can be partitioned into k nonvoind,
disjoint subsets, or parts, in such a way that no edge has both ends in the same part
and any two vertices in diﬀerent parts are adjacent.
We shall say that G is a complete multipartite graph if there is a cardinal number
k such that G is complete k-partite. It is easy to see G is complete multipartite if
and only if the non-adjacency is an equivalence relation on V (G) [30, p. 14].
Our next deﬁnition is a modiﬁcation of Deﬁnition 2.1 from [97 ].
Deﬁnition 4.8. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty metric space. Denote by GX,d a graph
such that V (GX,d) = X and, for u, v ∈ V (GX,d),
(4.9) ({u,v } ∈ E(GX,d)) ⇔ (d(u,v ) = diamX and u ̸=v).
We call GX,d the diametrical graph of (X,d ).
Example 4.9. If (X,d ) is an unbounded metric space or |X| = 1 holds, then the
diametrical graph GX,d is empty, E(GX,d) = ∅.
PAGE 15
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 15
Theorem 4.10. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space with |X|⩾ 2. Then the following
statements are equivalent:
(i) The diametrical graph GX,d of (X,d ) is nonempty.
(ii) The diametrical graph GX,d is complete multipartite.
(iii) There are some points x1, x2 ∈ X such that d(x1,x 2) = diam X.
Furthermore, if GX,d is complete multipartite, then every part of GX,d is an open
ball with a center in X and the radius equals diamX and, conversely, every open
ball Br(c) with r = diamX and c ∈ X is a part of GX,d.
Proof. The validity of (i) ⇔ (ii) follows from Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 of paper [32].
The equivalence (i) ⇔ (iii) is obviously true.
LetGX,d be a complete multipartite graph, let X1 be a part of GX,d and let x1 be
a point of X1. We claim that the equality
(4.10) X1 =Br(x1)
holds with r = diam X. Using Example 4.9, we see that the double inequality
0< diamX <∞ holds. Hence, the open ball Br(x1) is correctly deﬁned.
Let x2 be a point of the set X \X1. Since GX,d is complete multipartite and
x2 /∈ X1, the membership
(4.11) {x1,x 2} ∈ E(GX,d)
is valid. From (4.11) it follows that
d(x1,x 2) = diam X =r.
Hence, x2 ∈ X \Br(x1). Thus, the inclusion
(4.12) X \X1 ⊆ X \Br(x1)
holds.
Similarly, we can prove the inclusion X \Br(x1) ⊆ X \X1. The last inclusion and
(4.12) imply the equality
X \Br(x1) = X \X1.
This is equivalent to (4.10).
Let us consider now an open ball Br(c) with r = diam X and arbitrary c ∈ X.
Then there is a part X2 ofGX,d such thatc ∈ X2. Arguing as in the proof of equality
(4.10), we obtain the equality X2 =Br(c). □
Proposition 4.11. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space with |X|⩾ 2.
Then there is an integer k ⩾ 2 such that the diametrical graph GX,d is complete
k-partite.
Proof. First of all we claim that GX,d is nonempty.
Let ( ˜X, ˜d) be the completion of (X,d ). Then ( ˜X, ˜d) is a compact metric space. By
Corollary 3.12 and Theorem 4.10, the diametrical graph of co mpact metric space
is nonempty if this space contains at least two points. Conse quently, G ˜X, ˜d is a
nonempty graph. Now the last statement and Corollary 4.3 imp ly that GX,d is also
nonempty.
It is shown that the graph GX,d is nonempty. Hence, by Theorem 4.10, this graph
is complete multipartite. Consequently, there is a cardina l number k such that GX,d
PAGE 16
16 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
is complete k-partite. If k is an inﬁnite cardinal, then using Deﬁnitions 4.7 and 4.8
we can ﬁnd a sequence (pn)n∈ N ⊆ X such that
d(pn,p m) = diam(X,d )> 0
for all distinct n,m ∈ N. Hence, the sequence (pn)n∈ N does not contain any Cauchy
subsequence, that contradicts Proposition 3.5. □
Corollary 4.12. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space and let A be a
subset of X such that |A| ⩾ 2. Then the diametrical graph GA,d|A× A is complete
k-partite for an integer k ⩾ 2.
Proof. It follows from Corollary 3.6 and Proposition 4.11. □
Example 4.13. Let Z be the ring of all integer numbers and let

Bk =B1(k) ∩ Z, k = 0, 1,...,p − 1
(see the proof of Proposition 4.11 for details). It is intere sting to note that the
parts B0, ... , Bp− 1 are exactly the same as the residue classes (modulo p), i.e., the
equivalence
(x ∈ Bk) ⇔ (x ≡ k (mod p))
is valid for all x ∈ Z and k ∈ { 0, 1,...,p − 1}.

Theorem 4.17. Let (X,d ) be a metric space with |X| ⩾ 2. Then the following
statements are equivalent:
(i) The metric space (X,d ) is ultrametric.
(ii) Gr

(4.18) Ψr(t) = min {r,t }, t ∈ R+.

ofGr

5. The structure of distance sets and ultrametric preser ving
functions
In the ﬁrst part of the section, we mainly recall some basic fa cts related to the
theory of ordered sets. Proposition 5.10, which contains a c haracteristic property of
spherically complete ultrametric spaces, is the only new re sult of this part.
Using a method for constructing ultrametric spaces given by Delhommé, Laﬂamme,
Pouzet and Sauer, we completely specify the distance sets of inﬁnite totally bounded
ultrametric spaces and, respectively, of separable ultrametric spaces in Theorems 5.19
and 5.23 from the second part of the section.
The main new results of the third part of the section are Theor em 5.50, Theo-
rem 5.51 and Theorem 5.53 describing interrelations betwee n weak similarities of
ultrametric spaces and ultrametric (pseudoultrametric) p reserving functions. More-
over, in Theorems 5.29 and 5.41 with the help of complete mult ipartite graphs we
characterize bounded ultrametric spaces which are weakly s imilar to unbounded
ones. A similar characterization of totally bounded ultram etric spaces is given
in Theorem 5.62. Some characteristic properties of functio ns preserving totally
bounded ultrametrics are found in Theorem 5.59.
5.1. Basic facts about posets and isotone mappings
Let us start from the deﬁnition of order relation. A binary relation on a set Y is
a subset of the Cartesian square Y 2 =Y × Y of all ordered pairs ⟨x,y ⟩,x, y ∈ Y .
A binary relation R ⊆ Y 2 is reﬂexive if ⟨y,y ⟩ ∈ R holds for every y ∈ Y . As
usual, we say that R ⊆ Y 2 is a transitive binary relation if the transitive law
(⟨x,y ⟩ ∈ R and ⟨y,z ⟩ ∈ R) ⇒ (⟨x,z ⟩ ∈ R)
is valid for all x, y, z ∈ Y .
Recall that a reﬂexive and transitive binary relation ≼ onY is a partial order on
Y if ≼ has the antisymmetric property, the implication(
⟨x,y ⟩ ∈ ≼ and ⟨y,x ⟩ ∈ ≼
)
⇒ (x =y)
is valid for all x, y ∈ Y . In what follows we use the formula x ≼ y instead of
⟨x,y ⟩ ∈ ≼ and, in addition, x ≺ y and x ∥y mean that
x ≼y and x ̸=y
and, respectively, that
¬ (x ≼y) and ¬ (y ≼x).
Let ≼Y be a partial order on a set Y . A pair (Y, ≼Y ) is called to be a poset (a
partially ordered set). If Z is a subset of Y and ≼Z is a partial order on Z given
by ≼Z =Z 2 ∩ ≼Y , then we say that Z = (Z, ≼Z) is a subposet of (Y, ≼Y ). A poset
(Y, ≼Y ) is totally ordered if, for all points y1, y2 ∈ Y , we have
y1 ≼Y y2 or y2 ≼Y y1.
Let (P, ≼P ) be a poset. The totally ordered subposets of (P, ≼P ) are usually called
the chains in P . A chain C ⊆ P is said to be maximal (in P ) if the implication
(C ⊆ C1) ⇒ (C =C1)
is valid for every chain C1 ⊆ P .
The following proposition implies the existence of maximal chains in every nonempty
poset.
PAGE 19
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 19
Proposition 5.1. Let (P, ≼P ) be a nonempty poset and let C0 ⊆ P be a chain.
Then there is a maximal chain M ⊆ P such that C0 ⊆ M.
The proof of Proposition 5.1 can be obtained by standard appl ication of Zorn’s
Lemma (see Proposition 2.55 in [114]).
Deﬁnition 5.2. Let (Q, ≼Q) and (L, ≼L) be posets. A mapping f : Q → L is
isotone if, for all q1, q2 ∈ Q, we have
(q1 ≼Q q2) ⇒ (f (q1) ≼L f (q2)).
If, in addition, the isotone mapping f : Q → L is bijective and the inverse mapping
f − 1 : L → Q is also isotone, then we say that f is an order isomorphism and that
(Q, ≼Q) and (L, ≼L) have the same order type.
A mapping f : Q → L is strictly isotone if the implication
(5.1) (q1 ≺ Qq2) ⇒ (f (q1) ≺ L f (q2))
is valid for all q1, q2 ∈ Q.
It is clear that every strictly isotone mapping is isotone an d every order isomor-
phism is strictly isotone.
The following lemma will be useful in the sequel.
Lemma 5.3. Let (Q, ≼Q) and (L, ≼L) be posets and let f : Q → L be strictly isotone
and surjective. If the implication
(q1 ∥Qq2) ⇒ (f (q1) ∥Lf (q2))
is valid for all q1, q2 ∈ Q, then f is an order isomorphism.
For the proof see, for example, Theorem 9.3 in [65, p. 35].
Corollary 5.4. Let (Q, ≼Q) be totally ordered. Then, for every poset (L, ≼L), each
strictly isotone surjection f : Q → L is an order isomorphism.
Deﬁnition 5.5. Let (P, ≼P ) be a poset and let S ⊆ P . Then p ∈ P is called an
upper bound of S if the inequality s ≼P p holds for every s ∈ S. The point l ∈ P
is called the lowest upper bound or join of S if l is an upper bound of S and the
inequalityl ≼P p holds for every upper bound p of S.
The concepts of the lower bound and the greatest lower bound or meet can be
deﬁned by duality.
For arbitrary subset A of arbitrary poset (P, ≼P ) we will denote the join and the
meet of A by ∨A and by ∧A, respectively. Furthermore, we will write
supA = ∨A and infA = ∧A
for the case when A is a subset of the poset (R, ⩽).
Let us recall now the concept of the largest element of poset.
Deﬁnition 5.6. Let (P, ≼P ) be a partially ordered set. An element l ∈ P is called
the largest element of P if we have x ≼P l for every x ∈ P . We will also say that
m ∈ P is a maximal element ofP if, for every y ∈ P , the inequality m ≼P y implies
the equality m =y.
It is easy to see that if l is the largest element of (P, ≼P ), then l is a maximal
element of (P, ≼P ), but not conversely in general.
PAGE 20
20 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Example 5.7. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty metric space with a distance set D(X). Let
us consider (D(X), ⩽) as a subposet of (R, ⩽). Then the join of D(X) exists if and
only if X is bounded. For bounded (X,d ) the equality
diamX = ∨D(X)
holds. Moreover, diamX ∈ D(X) holds if and only if (D(X), ⩽) contains a largest
elementl and in this case we have l = diamX.
Example 5.8. Let (P, ≼P ) be a poset and let Ch = Ch P be the set of all totally
ordered subposets of (P, ≼P ). Let us deﬁne a partial order ≼Ch on Ch as
(C1 ≼Ch C2) ⇔ (C1 ⊆ C2).
Then (Ch, ≼Ch) has the largest element if and only if (P, ≼P ) is a chain.
It is well-known that the completeness of the real line R with respect to the
standard Euclidean metric is equivalent to the existence of supA and infA for every
nonempty bounded A ⊆ R. In the last section of the paper, we will use a similar
interconnection to construct a compact ultrametric space c orresponding to a poset
of vertices of locally ﬁnite tree with monotone labeling.

Remark 5.11. For the ultrametric spaces, the equivalence of the spherica l complete-
ness to the fulﬁllment of condition (ii) of Proposition 5.10 is known, but usually this
fact is formulated without using the language of the theory o f ordered sets (see, for
example, Theorem 1 [81]).
Using Proposition 5.10 and equalities (5.3) we also obtain t he following.
Corollary 5.12. An ultrametric space (X,d ) is spherically complete if and only if
the met ∧ A exists for every nonempty chain A of the poset (
BX, ≼X)
Remark 5.13. The spherical complete ultrametric spaces were ﬁrst introd uced by
Ingleton [76] in order to obtain an analog of the Hanh—Banach theorem for non-
Archimedean valued ﬁelds. This notion has numerous applica tions in studies of
ﬁxed point results for ultrametric spaces [19, 68, 80, 101]. It was shown by Bayod
and Martínez-Maurica [6] that an ultrametric space is spher ically complete if and
only if this space is ultrametrically injective. Recall tha t an ultrametric space (Y,ρ )
is ultrametrically injective if for each F : A → X, where A ⊆ Y and X is a space
with an ultrametric d, the condition
d(F (x),F (y)) ⩽ρ(x,y ), ∀x,y ∈ A
implies the existence of an extension ˆF : Y → X of the mapping F such that
d( ˆF (x), ˆF (y)) ⩽ρ(x,y ), ∀x,y ∈ Y.
Thus, an ultrametric space is ultrametrically injective if every contractive mapping
from this space to arbitrary ultrametric space has a contrac tive extension. Some
interesting results related to spherical completeness of u ltrametric spaces can also
be found in [113] and [81].
5.2. Distance sets of ultrametric spaces
The following general fact was proved in Proposition 2 of pap er [28].
Theorem 5.14. LetA be a subset of R+. Then A is the distance set of a nonempty
ultrametric space if and only if 0 belongs to A.
Below we reﬁne this theorem for totally bounded ultrametric spaces and for sep-
arable ones.
Lemma 5.15. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded inﬁnite ultrametric space. Then D(X)
is countably inﬁnite, |D(X)|= ℵ 0.
Proof. Every totally bounded metric space is separable. Consequen tly,X contains
an at most countable dense subset A. Thus, we have the inequalities
(5.8) |D(A,d |A× A)|⩽ |A × A|⩽ ℵ 0.
Corollary 4.3 and (5.8) imply that
|D(X)|= |D(A,d |A× A)|⩽ ℵ 0.
Suppose |D(X)|is a ﬁnite number, |D(X)|< ℵ 0. Since X is inﬁnite, the inequality
|D(X)| ⩾ 2 holds. The double inequality 2 ⩽ |D(X)|< ℵ 0 implies that there is
d1 ∈ D(X) such that
(5.9) 0<d 1 ⩽d∗
PAGE 23
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 23
for every d∗ ∈ D(X) \ {0}. Let us consider the family
F =
{
Bd1/2(x) :x ∈ X
}
.
Then F is a cover of X,
X ⊆
⋃
B∈F
B.
Using (5.9) we obtain Bd1/2(x) ∩ Bd1/2(y) = ∅ for all distinct x, y ∈ X. Hence, the
cover F does not contain any ﬁnite subcover. This contradicts Deﬁni tion 3.3. The
equality |D(X)|= ℵ 0 follows. □
Lemma 5.16. Let (X,d ) be a compact ultrametric space with the distance set D(X)
and let p > 0 belong to D(X). Then p is an isolated point of the metric space
(D(X),ρ ) with the standard metric ρ(x,y ) = |x − y|.
Proof. Suppose for every ε> 0 there is s ∈ D(X) satisfying
(5.10) p<s<p +ε.
Then there is (sn)n∈ N ⊆ D(X) such that
(5.11) lim

for every n ∈ N. We can ﬁnd two sequences (xn)n∈ N ⊆ X and (yn)n∈ N ⊆ X such

(5.12) d(xn,y n) = sn
for every n ∈ N and
(5.13) lim

By Bolzano—Weierstrass property (Proposition 3.2), there are points x,y ∈ X and
subsequences (xnk )k∈ N of (xn)n∈ N and (ynk)k∈ N of (yn)n∈ N such that
(5.14) lim

Since d : X × X → R+ is continuous, from (5.11), (5.12) and (5.14) it follows tha t
d(x,y ) = p.
Let us consider the cycle Ck with

Thus, there is ε1 > 0 such that

Arguing similarly we can ﬁnd ε2 > 0 satisfying
(5.16) (p − ε2,p ) ∩ D(X) = ∅.
From (5.15) and (5.16) it follows that p is an isolated point of D(X). □

Deﬁnition 5.17. A poset has the order type 1 + ω∗ if this poset is order isomorphic
to the subposet
{0} ∪
{ 1
n : n ∈ N
}
of the poset (R, ⩽), where ⩽ is the standard order on R.
Remark 5.18. The symbol ω is the usual designation of the order type of (N, ⩽)
and 1 denotes the order type of one-point posets. The symbol ω∗ is used for the
backwards order type of ω. Moreover, by 1 + ω∗ we denote the sum of the order
types 1 and ω∗ (see, for example, Section 1.4 in [109] for details).
It was noted in Theorem 5.14 that a set A ⊆ R+ is the distance set of a nonempty
ultrametric space if and only if 0 ∈ A. For totally bounded ultrametric spaces this
result admits the following reﬁnement.
Theorem 5.19. LetA be a subset of R+. Then A is the distance set of an inﬁnite
totally bounded ultrametric space if and only if the followi ng conditions simultane-
ously hold:
(i) 0 ∈ A.
(ii) The poset (A, ⩽A) with ⩽A =A2 ∩ ⩽ has the order type 1 + ω∗.
(iii) The point 0 is an accumulation point of the set A with respect to the stan-
dard Euclidean metric on R.
Proof. Let X be an inﬁnite totally bounded ultrametric space with A = D(X).
Then from X ̸= ∅ it follows that 0 ∈ A. Let us prove the validity of (ii).
The completion ˜X of X is compact (see Proposition 3.8) and ultrametric (see
Lemma 3.9). By Corollary 4.4, the equality D(X) = D( ˜X) holds. Hence, without
loss generality, we assume that X is compact.
By Lemma 5.15, we have |D(X)|= ℵ 0. Consequently, it suﬃces to show that the
cardinality of
Dd∗ =D(X) ∩ [d∗, diamX],
where [d∗, diamX] = {s ∈ R+ : d∗ ⩽s ⩽ diamX}, is ﬁnite.
By Lemma 3.11, the distance set D(X) is a compact subset of R. The classical
Heine—Borel theorem (from Real Analysis) claims that a subs et S of R is compact
if and only if S is closed and bounded. Thus, Dd∗ is compact. By Lemma 5.16,
everyp ∈ Dd∗ is isolated. Hence, for every p ∈ Dd∗ , the one-point set {p} is an open
ball in the metric subspace Dd∗ of R (see Proposition 2.5). It is clear that the open
cover {
{p} : p ∈ Dd∗
}
contains a ﬁnite subcover if and only if the cardinal number |Dd∗ | is ﬁnite. Now
the inequality |Dd∗ |< ∞ follows from Borel—Lebesgue property (Deﬁnition 3.1).
Condition (ii) follows.
Let us prove condition (iii). Using Proposition 4.4 and Lemma 3.11 we obtain
thatA is a compact subset of R+. If (iii) does not hold, then 0 is an isolated point of
A. Consequently, by Lemma 5.16, all points of A are isolated. Since A is compact,
it implies the ﬁniteness of A, contrary to (ii). Condition (iii) follows.
Conversely, suppose that A satisﬁes conditions (i)– (iii). As in Example 2.12,
we write A = X and deﬁne the ultrametric d : X × X → R+ by (2.5). Then the
equality A = D(X) follows from the deﬁnition of d. Using (i)– (iii), it is easy to
prove that (X,d ) is compact, ultrametric and inﬁnite. □

Remark 5.20. It can be shown that characteristic properties of the distan ce sets
of inﬁnite totally bounded ultrametric spaces presented by conditions (i)– (iii) of
Theorem 5.19 are independent of one another.
Example 5.21. Let (xn)n∈ N ⊆ R+ be a strictly decreasing sequence such that
lim

(x ∈ A) ⇔ (x = 0 or ∃n ∈ N : x =xn).

is a compact, inﬁnite ultrametric space satisfying the equa lityD(X,d ) = A.
Example 5.21 gives us a “constructive” description of dista nce sets of all inﬁnite
totally bounded ultrametric spaces. For compact ultrametr ic spaces, the following
reformulation of Theorem 5.19 can be found in [113] Proposit ion 19.2.
Corollary 5.22. The following statements are equivalent for every A ⊆ R+:
(i) There is an inﬁnite compact ultrametric space X such that A =D(X).
(ii) There is an inﬁnite totally bounded ultrametric space X such that A =
D(X).
(iii) There is a strictly decreasing sequence (xn)n∈ N ⊆ R+ such that
lim

We conclude the second part of the section by following modiﬁ cation of Theo-
rem 5.14 for separable ultrametric spaces.
Proposition 5.23. Let A be a subset of R+. Then A is the distance set of a
nonempty separable ultrametric space if and only if 0 ∈ A and |A|⩽ ℵ 0 hold.
For the proof it suﬃces to note that |D(X)| ⩽ ℵ 0 holds for every separable
ultrametric space X (see inequality (5.8) in the proof of Lemma 5.15) and use the
Delhommé—Laﬂamme—Pouzet—Sauer construction which was de scribed in Exam-
ple 2.12.
Example 5.24. Let p ⩾ 2 be a prime number. The ultrametric space (Qp,d p) is a
completion of the countable ultrametric space (Q,d p). Hence, (Qp,d p) is separable.
The distance sets D(Q,d p) and D(Qp,d p) are both equal to the set
{pn : n ∈ Z} ∪ { 0}
of all integer powers of p together with the added zero (see, for example, [60, p. 58]).
Remark 5.25. Theorem 5.19, Corollary 5.22 and Proposition 5.23 can be fou nd in
paper [54] that contains also some results describing the di stance sets of ultrametric
spaces with the same topology. It was proved by A. J. Lemin and V. Lemin [87]
that, for every inﬁnite, ultrametric space X, the cardinality of D(X) is no greater
than the weight of X,
(5.17) |D(X)|⩽w(X).
PAGE 26
26 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
In particular, (5.17) implies |D(X)|⩽ ℵ 0 for all separable ultrametric spaces X. For
the case of ﬁnite ultrametric space X, we evidently have the equality w(X) = |X|
and, consequently, inequality (5.17) turns into the Gomory —Hu inequality
|D(X)|⩽ |X|,
which was deduced R. Gomory and T. Hu [59] by studying the ﬂows in networks.
The necessary and suﬃcient conditions under which the group of self-isometries of
ultrametric space X that can be represented by some labeled star graph S coincides
with the group of self-isomorphisms of S are also determined by properties of D(X)
[53].
5.3. Ultrametric preserving functions and weak similarities
A function f : R+ → R+ is called ultrametric preserving if
X × X
d
− →R+ f
− →R+
is an ultrametric on X for every ultrametric space (X,d ).
The following elegant result was obtained by Pongsriiam and Termwuttipong in
paper [100].
Theorem 5.26. The following conditions are equivalent for every f : R+ → R+:
(i) f is ultrametric preserving.
(ii) f is increasing and the equality f (x) = 0 holds if and only if x = 0.
Some facts related to ultrametric preserving functions can be found in [12, 14, 38–
40, 100, 111, 126].
Example 5.27. Let f : R+ → R+ be deﬁned as
(5.18) f (t) = d∗t
1 +t
withd∗ ∈ (0, ∞ ). Then f is increasing and f (t) = 0 holds if and only if t = 0. Hence,
f is ultrametric preserving by Theorem 5.26. Moreover, f ◦d is a metric for every
metric space (X,d ) (Example 2 in [31]). Thus, f is also metric preserving . Using
the function f it is easy to show that, for every unbounded metric space (Y,ρ ), the
metric space (Y,δ ) with δ =f ◦ρ is bounded and has the same topology as (Y,ρ ).
In Theorem 5.29 below we will use the transformation d ↦→ f ◦d withf : R+ → R+
deﬁned by equality (5.18) to characterize the bounded ultra metric spaces with empty
diametrical graphs.
The above formulated Pongsriiam—Termwuttipong theorem ca n be partially gen-
eralized as follows.
Lemma 5.28. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty ultrametric space with the distance set
D(X) and let f : D(X) → R+ be an isotone mapping from the poset (D(X), ⩽) to
the poset (R+, ⩽). Then the mapping
X × X ∋ ⟨x,y ⟩ ↦→ f (d(x,y )) ∈ R+
is an ultrametric on X if and only if the equation f (t) = 0 has the unique solution
t = 0.
Proof. It directly follows form Theorem 5.6 [126]. □

Remark 5.30. The condition |X| ⩾ 2 cannot be dropped in the second part of

In the case of strictly increasing ultrametric preserving f : R+ → R+, the tran-
sition from (X,d ) to (X,f ◦d) can also be described using the concept of weak
similarity.
Deﬁnition 5.31. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty metric spaces. A mapping
Φ :X → Y is a weak similarity of (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) if Φ is bijective and there is a
strictly increasing function f : D(Y ) → D(X) such that the following diagram
(5.24)

Remark 5.32. In Diagram (5.24) and other diagrams illustrating the appli cation of
weak similarities to metric spaces we write Φ ⊗ Φ for the mapping
X × X ∋ ⟨x,y ⟩ ↦→ ⟨ Φ(x), Φ(y)⟩ ∈ Y × Y.
Moreover, for every metric d on X we use the symbol d : X × X → D(X) for the
surjection induced by restricting codomain R+ of d to its range D(X).
If Φ :X → Y is a weak similarity, f : D(Y ) → D(X) is strictly increasing, and
(5.25) is fulﬁlled for all x, y ∈ X, then we say that (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) are weakly
similar, and f is the scaling function of Φ.
Remark 5.33. It follows directly from Deﬁnition 5.31 that the scaling fun ction is
uniquely determined by given weak similarity. Moreover, Co rollary 5.4 implies that
every scaling function is an order isomorphism of correspon ding distance sets.
Example 5.34. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty metric spaces. A mapping Φ :X →
Y is a similarity, if Φ is bijective and there is a strictly positive number r = r(Φ),
the ratio of Φ, such that

=rd(x,y )
for all x, y ∈ X (see, for example, [55, p. 45]). It is clear that every isomet ry is a
similarity with the ratior = 1 and every similarity is a weak similarity. Furthermore,
a weak similarity Φ :X → Y with the scaling function f : D(Y ) → D(X) is an
isometry if and only if f (t) = t holds for every t ∈ D(Y ).
Example 5.35. Let Φ :X → Y be a bijection and let d : X ×X → R+ andρ : Y ×Y →
R+ be some metrics. The mapping Φ is a weak similarity of (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) if and
only if the equivalence
(5.26) (d(x,y ) ⩽d(w,z )) ⇔ (ρ(Φ(x), Φ(y)) ⩽ρ(Φ(w), Φ(z)))
is valid for all x, y, z, w ∈ X.
Remark 5.36. Equivalence (5.26) evidently implies the validity of
(5.27) (d(x,y ) = d(w,z )) ⇔ (ρ(Φ(x), Φ(y)) = ρ(Φ(w), Φ(z))).

ultrametric and semimetric spaces were also considered by E . Petrov in [95].

Lemma 5.37 implies the following.
Corollary 5.38. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty weakly similar ultrametric spaces.
Then the diametrical graph GX,d is empty if and only if the diametrical graph GY,ρ
is empty.
Proof. By Lemma 5.37, the posets (D(X), ⩽) and (D(Y ), ⩽) are order isomorphic.
Hence, (D(X), ⩽) has largest element if and only if (D(Y ), ⩽) has the largest ele-
ment. To complete the proof it suﬃces to remember that the lar gest element of the
distance set of metric space, if such an element exists, coin cides with the diameter
of the space (see Example 5.7). □
Remark 5.39. For more detailed proof of Lemma 5.37 in more general case of s emi-
metric spaces see Proposition 1.2 in [47].

(i) The function f is strictly increasing.

are weakly similar.

Using the concept of weak similarity we can give a more compac t variant of

Theorem 5.41. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space with |X|⩾ 2. Then the following

(i) (X,d ) is weakly similar to an unbounded ultrametric space.

Recall that a topological space (X,τ ) is connected if it is not the union of two
disjoint nonempty open subsets of X. A subset S ofX is said to be connected if S is
connected as subspace of (X,τ ). A component (= connected component) of (X,τ )
is, by deﬁnition, a connected set A ⊆ X for which the implication
(S ⊇ A) ⇒ (S =A)
is valid for every connected S ⊆ X. The set of all components of nonempty (X,τ )
forms a partition of X. Thus, for every x ∈ X, there exists a unique component
C(x) of (X,τ ) such that x ∈ C(x).
Lemma 5.42. A nonempty subset S of the real line R is connected if and only if S
is an interval.
For the proof see Proposition 4 in [20, p. 336].
Remark 5.43. By bounded interval in R we mean any subset of R which can be
given in the one of the following forms:
[a,b ] = {x ∈ R : a ⩽x ⩽b}, (a,b ] = {x ∈ R : a<x ⩽b},
[a,b ) = {x ∈ R : a ⩽x<b }, (a,b ) = {x ∈ R : a<x<b },
where a, b ∈ R with a ⩽b for [a,b ] and a < bfor (a,b ], [a,b ) and (a,b ). Similarly,
by deﬁnition, any unbounded interval in R has one of the forms:
(−∞ , ∞ ) = R, [a, ∞ ) = {x ∈ R : x ⩾a},
(a, ∞ ) = {x ∈ R : x>a }, (−∞ ,a ] = {x ∈ R : x ⩽a}
or (−∞ ,a ) = {x ∈ R : x<a } with arbitrary a ∈ R.
Remark 5.44. In all topological spaces every single-point set and the emp ty set are
connected ([20, p. 108]). Moreover, if (X,τ ) is a connected topological space, then
the set X is the unique component of (X,τ ). In particular, the empty set ∅ is a
component of (X,τ ) if and only if X = ∅.
Lemma 5.45. Let A be a nonempty subset of R. Then all components of A are
intervals in R.
Proof. Letp be a point of A. Then we denote by C(p) the connected component of
A containing the point p.
Suppose contrary that there is a point p ∈ A for which the component C(p) is
not an interval in R. Then, by Lemma 5.42, the set C(p) is not a connected subset
of R. A subset E of R is connected if and only if [x,y ] ⊆ E holds whenever x,
y ∈ E andx<y (see, for example, Theorem 2.47 [110]). Consequently, ther e are x,
y ∈ C(p) and z ∈ R \C(p) such that x<z <y . Then the sets (−∞ ,z ) ∩ C(p) and
C(p) ∩ (z, ∞ ) are disjoint nonempty open subsets of the topological space C(p) and
we have the equality
C(p) = (( −∞ ,z ) ∩ C(p)) ∪ (C(p) ∩ (z, ∞ )).
Hence, C(p) is not a connected subset of A, that contradicts the deﬁnition of con-
nected components. □

32 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
The useful generalization of the concept of ultrametric is t he concept of pseudoul-
trametric.
Deﬁnition 5.46. LetX be a set and let d : X × X → R+ be a symmetric function
such that d(x,x ) = 0 holds for every x ∈ X. The function d is a pseudoultrametric
on X if it satisﬁes the strong triangle inequality.
Ifd is a pseudoultrametric onX, then we will say that (X,d ) is a pseudoultrametric
space.
Every ultrametric space is a pseudoultrametric space but no t conversely. In
contrast to ultrametric spaces, pseudoultrametric spaces can contain some distinct
points with zero distance between them.
Deﬁnition 5.47. A function f : R+ → R+ is pseudoultrametric preserving if f ◦d
is a pseudoultrametric for every pseudoultrametric space (X,d ).
The following result is a continuation of the Pongsriiam—Te rmwuttipong theorem
to the case of pseudoultrametric preserving functions.
Proposition 5.48. The following conditions are equivalent for every f : R+ → R+:
(i) The function f is increasing and f (0) = 0 holds.
(ii) The function f is pseudoultrametric preserving.
For the proof see Proposition 2.4 [38].
Remark 5.49. Theorem 5.26 and Proposition 5.48 imply that every ultramet ric pre-
serving function is pseudoultrametric preserving. Moreov er, a pseudoultrametric
preservingf : R+ → R+ is ultrametric preserving if and only if f − 1(0) = {0} holds.
Using pseudoultrametric preserving functions we can now ob tain a new description
of bounded ultrametric spaces with empty diametrical graph s.
In what follows we will use the symbol R+ \D(X) to denote the relative comple-
ment of the distance set D(X) of an ultrametric space X with respect to R+.
Theorem 5.50. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space with |X| ⩾ 2 and let D(X) be
the distance set of (X,d ). Then the following conditions are equivalent.
(i) The space (X,d ) is bounded and the diametrical graph GX,d is empty.
(ii) There is a ∈ (0, ∞ ) such that the set [a, ∞ ) is a component of R+ \D(X).
(iii) There is an ultrametric space (Z,δ ) such that (Z,δ ) and (X,d ) are weakly
similar, but if Φ :Z → X is a weak similarity and ψ : D(X) → D(Z) is the
scaling function of Φ, then
(5.29) g|D(X) ̸=ψ
holds for every pseudoultrametric preserving function g : R+ → R+.
Proof. (i) ⇔ (ii). Let (i) hold. The inequality |X|⩾ 2 implies the strict inequality
diamX >0. Since (X,d ) is bounded and the diametrical graph GX,d is empty, the
double inequality
0< diamX <∞
and the inclusion
[diamX, ∞ ) ⊆ R+ \D(X)
take place. It follows from the equality
diamX = supD(X)

that [diamX, ∞ ) is the maximal interval in R+ \D(X) which contains the point
diamX. Hence, [diamX, ∞ ) is a component of R+ \D(X) by Lemma 5.45. Thus,
(ii) follows.
Let a be a point of (0, ∞ ) and let [a, ∞ ) be a component of R+ \D(X). By
Lemma 5.45, [a,b ) is the maximal interval in R+ \D(X) contained the point a.
Hence, the intersection (a − ε,a ) ∩D(X) is nonempty for every ε ∈ (0,a ). It implies
the equalities
a = supD(X) = diam X.
(i) ⇔ (iii). Let (i) hold. By Theorem 5.41, there is an unbounded ultrametric
space (Z,δ ) such that (X,d ) and (Z,δ ) are weakly similar. Let Φ :Z → X be a weak
similarity and let ψ : D(X) → D(Z) be the scaling function of Φ. Since (Z,δ ) is
unbounded and ψ is surjective, the set ψ (D(X)) is unbounded. Now if g : R+ → R+
is an arbitrary pseudoultrametric preserving function, th en we have the inequality
g(t) ⩽g(diamX)
for every t ∈ D(X), because g is increasing. Thus,
g(D(X)) ⊆ [0,g (diamX)]
holds. The last statement and unboundedness of ψ (D(X)) imply (5.29).
Let condition (iii) hold. If (i) does not hold, then either (X,d ) is unbounded or
(X,d ) is bounded and has the nonempty diametrical graph GX,d.
Consider ﬁrst the case when (X,d ) is unbounded. By condition (iii), there is
an ultrametric space (Z,δ ) such that (X,d ) and (Z,δ ) are weakly similar, and if
Φ :Z → X is a weak similarity with a scaling function ψ , then (5.29) holds for every
pseudoultrametric preserving g. Let us deﬁne a function f : R+ → R+ as
(5.30) f (t) = sup {ψ (s) :s ∈ [0,t ] ∩ D(X)}, t ∈ R+.
Then f is increasing and satisﬁes the equality f (0) = 0 . By Proposition 5.48, the
function f is pseudoultrametric preserving. Moreover, from (5.30) it follows the
equality
(5.31) f |D(X) =ψ.
The last equality contradicts (5.29) for g =f .
Reasoning in completely similar way, we can prove the validi ty of equality (5.31)
in the case when (X,d ) is bounded and has nonempty GX,d, which completes the
proof of the theorem. □

Corollary 5.54. Let X and Z be nonempty weakly similar ultrametric spaces and
let the distance set D(X) be a closed subset of R+. Then, for every weak similarity
Φ :Z → X, the scaling function of Φ admits a continuation to a strictly increasing
ultrametric preserving function.
Proof. It follows from Theorem 5.53 because the set R+ \D(X) is an open subset of
R and every component of any nonempty open subset of R is an open interval. □
Corollary 5.55. Let X be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space. Then,
for every weak similarity Φ :Z → X of an ultrametric space Z and the ultrametric
spaceX, there is a strictly increasing ultrametric preserving fun ction g : R+ → R+
such that
g|D(X) =ψ,
whereψ : D(X) → D(Z) is the scaling function of Φ.
Proof. It follows from Corollary 5.54 and Corollary 5.22. □

Theorems 5.50–5.53 can be considered as reformulations of s ome results guaran-
teeing, in the language of ultrametric spaces, the existenc e of isotonic extension of
given isotonic mappings. For example, Theorem 5.50 is a simp le corollary of the
following.
Theorem 5.57. Let D be a subset of R+ such that 0 ∈ D. Then the following
conditions are equivalent:
(i) For every strictly increasing function ψ : D → R+ with ψ (0) = 0 there is
an increasing function g : R+ → R+ such that g|D =ψ .
(ii) The poset (D, ⩽) has a largest element or D is a conﬁnal subset of (R+, ⩽).
The above theorem admits also future generalizations for pa rtially ordered sets
and, in particular, for diﬀerent types of semilattices and l attices [9,24,37,58,61,120].
We note only that lattices are a special kind of posets which p lay a fundamental
role in the theory of ultrametric spaces [86].
The next our goal is to ﬁnd a reﬁnement of the Pongsriiam—Term wuttipong
theorem for totally bounded ultrametric spaces.
Deﬁnition 5.58. Let (X,d ) and (X,ρ ) be metric spaces. The metrics d and ρ are
(topologically) equivalent if these metrics generate one and the same topology,
(A is open in (X,d )) ⇔ (A is open in (X,ρ ))
for every A ⊆ X.

The following result can be considered as a special case of Th eorem 29 from [39].
Theorem 5.59. The following conditions are equivalent for every ψ : R+ → R+:
(i) ψ is ultrametric preserving and continuous at 0.

on X such that ρ and d are topologically equivalent.

Example 5.60. A valuation of the ﬁeld Q is a mapping | · |: Q → R+ satisfying the
following conditions:
(i) |x|= 0 holds if and only if x = 0;
(ii) |x +y|⩽ |x|+ |y|;
(iii) |xy|= |x| · |y|
for all x, y ∈ Q. A valuation | · |is trivial if we have |x|= 1 wheneverx ̸= 0. Every
valuation | · |: Q → R+ generates a metric d : Q × Q → R+ by the rule
d(x,y ) = |x − y|.
PAGE 42
42 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
If metrics d : Q × Q → R+ and ρ : Q × Q → R+ are topologically equivalent and
generated by some valuations on Q, then there is α ∈ (0, ∞ ) such that
ρ(x,y ) = dα(x,y )
for all x, y ∈ Q (for the proof see, for example, Theorem 9.2 in [113]).
The famous Ostrovski Theorem claims that every metricd : Q× Q → R+ generated
by nontrivial valuation | · |: Q → R+ is topologically equivalent either to standard
Euclidean metric on Q or to some p-adic metric dp : Q × Q → R+.
Remark 5.61. Theorem 5.59 can be considered as a modiﬁcation of correspon ding
results for general metric spaces. In this connection see, i n particular, Theorem 3.3
in [18], Theorem 2.2 in [67] and Theorem 2.8 in [44].

Let us now look at another interesting application of ultram etric preserving func-

Proposition 5.65. Let (X,d ) be a bounded, non-discrete, ultrametric space and let
ε> 0, K >1 be given. Then there is an ultrametric ρ : X × X → R+ such that:
(i) The identity mapping id : (X,d ) → (X,ρ ) is non-expanding andK-Lipschitz,

Remark 5.66. One of the central concepts of the present section, the conce pt of
weak similarity, was recently introduced in [47], but this i s closely related to the
ordinal scaling, multidimensional scaling and ranking tha t appear naturally in many
applied researches [1, 17, 77, 82, 83, 107, 108, 116, 117, 130 ]. The presence of these
relationships and, thus, the existence of potential applic ations, makes the study of
weak similarities more important and promising.
6. The first look on ultrametric balleans
In this section we prove the inclusion BX ⊆ BX for all totally bounded ultrametric
spaces (X,d ) (see Corollary 6.2) and ﬁnd the conditions under which BX = BX
holds (see Corollary 6.8). Moreover, Proposition 6.12 desc ribes some interrelations
between the ballean BX of ultrametric space (X,d ) and the ballean BY of dense
Y ⊆ X, that implies the order isomorphism of balleans of ultramet ric spaces which
have isometric completions, Theorem 6.13.
Recall that if A is a nonempty, bounded subset in an ultrametric space (X,d ),
then there is the smallest ball B∗ ∈ BX containingA (see Proposition 2.17).
Proposition 6.1. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space, let B ∈ BX
and let B∗ ∈ BX be the smallest ball containing B. Then the equality
(6.1) B =B∗
holds.
Proof. By Deﬁnition 2.16, we have the inclusion B∗ ⊇ B. Thus, (6.1) holds if
(6.2) B∗ ⊆ B.
Inclusion (6.2) is trivially valid if |B|= 1. Let B =Br(c) and let |B|⩾ 2 hold. By
Corollary 4.12, the diametrical graph GB,d is complete multipartite. Hence, GB,d is
nonempty and there is b ∈ B such that
diamB =d(b,c ).
Writer∗ = diamB. Proposition 2.17 implies the equality B∗ =
Br∗ (c). Since b ∈ B,
we have the strict inequality r∗ <r . Inclusion (6.2) follows. □
Corollary 6.2. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space. Then the inclusion
(6.3) BX ⊆ BX
holds.
Proof. Let B ∈ BX. By Proposition 2.17, there is the smallest ball B∗ ∈
BX
containingB. Proposition 6.1 implies the equality B =B∗. Hence, we have B ∈ BX.
Inclusion (6.3) follows. □
The inclusion BX ⊆ BX can be false if ultrametric space (X,d ) is not totally
bounded.
Example 6.3. As in Example 2.12, let X = [0, 1] and let d be the ultrametric deﬁned
by formula (2.5). Write

(6.4) A =Br∗ (c∗) = {x ∈ X : d(x,c ∗) ⩽r∗}.
Using Theorem 5.19, we can ﬁnd ε> 0 such that the annulus
{x ∈ X : r∗ <d (x,c ∗)<r ∗ +ε}
is empty. The last statement and (6.4) imply
A = {x ∈ X : d(x,c ∗)<r ∗ +ε} =Br∗ +ε(c∗).
Thus, the membership relation A ∈ BX is valid. □
Corollary 6.5. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space, B1 ∈
BX and B1 ̸=X. Then there is B2 ∈ BX such that B1 is a part of the diametrical
graphGB2,d|B2× B2
.
Proof. Let b1 be a point of B1 and let
(6.5) Db1 = {d(x,b 1) :x ∈ X}.
It is clear that Db1 is a subset of the distance set D(X). By Proposition 2.6,
there is r1 > 0 such that B1 = Br1(b1). Condition B1 ̸= X implies diamX ⩾ r1.
Using equality (2.2) with A = X and a = b1 we obtain supDb1 = diam X. By
Proposition 4.11, there are some points a1, c1 ∈ X for which
(6.6) d(a1,c 1) = diam X
holds. Now from the strong triangle inequality
max{d(a1,b 1),d (b1,c 1)} ⩾d(a1,c 1)
and (6.6) it follows that
diamX ∈ Db1.
Consequently, the set [r1, diamX]∩Db1 is nonempty. Theorem 5.19 and the inequal-
ityr1 > 0 imply that the subposet [r1, diamX] ∩ Db1 of (R, ⩽) contains a smallest
element. Let us consider the closed ball
(6.7) B2 =
Br2(b1)
where r2 is the smallest element of [r1, diamX] ∩ Db1. Since r1 > 0 and r2 ⩾ r1,
we have r2 > 0. From r2 ∈ Db1 and equalities (6.5), (6.7) it follows that diamB2 =
r2 > 0. Hence, B2 ∈ BX by Lemma 6.4. From the deﬁnition of r2 it follows that
(6.8) B1 =Br1(b1) = Br2(b1).

46 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
By Theorem 4.10 (with X =B1), equalities (6.7) and (6.8) imply that B1 is a part
of GB2,d|B2× B2
. □
Remark 6.6. It was shown in the proof of Corollary 6.5 that the inequality
(6.9) diamB2 > diamB1
holds but, in general, we can have diamB1 = diamB2 for B1, B2 ∈ BX, where B1
is a part of GB2,d|B2× B2
.
Example 6.7. Let (X,d ) be the separable ultrametric space from Example 2.12.
Then the diametrical graph GX,d is complete bipartite (more speciﬁcally GX,d is a
star having a countable many leaves) with the parts
X1 = [0, 1) ∩ Q and X2 = {1}.
Hence, the equality diamX = diamX1 holds.
Using Lemma 6.4 we also can simply describe the totally bound ed ultrametric
spaces (X,d ) for which the inclusion BX ⊆
BX turns into equality.
Corollary 6.8. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space. Then the equality
(6.10) BX = BX
holds if and only if all points of X are isolated.
Proof. If all points of X are isolated, then we evidently have
{x} ∈ BX and {x} ∈ BX
for every x ∈ X. This statement and Lemma 6.4 yield equality (6.10).
Conversely, suppose X contains an accumulation point x∗. Then the closed ball
{x∗} =B0(x∗) belongs to BX but {x∗} /∈ BX. Thus, BX ̸= BX holds. □
Deﬁnition 6.9. A topological space (X,τ ) is said to be discrete if every subset of
X is open. We say that a metric space (Y,ρ ) is discrete if the topology generated
by metric ρ is discrete.
It is easy to see that a metric space is discrete if and only if e very point of this
space is isolated. Thus, by Corollary 6.8, the equality BX = BX holds for totally
bounded ultrametric space X if and only if this is a discrete space.
Example 6.10. Let X = [0, 1] ⊆ R and let d : X × X → R+ be deﬁned as in (2.5).
Then the ultrametric space (X,d ) is bounded and complete but not totally bounded.
Using Theorem 5.19 we can simply characterize all compact su bspaces of (X,d ).
Every ﬁnite subset of X is compact. An inﬁnite Y ⊆ X is compact if and only if
0 ∈ Y , the equality inf(Y \ {0}) = 0 holds, and the order type of (Y, ⩽Y ) is 1 + ω∗,
where ⩽Y = ⩽ ∩ Y 2. Consequently, every inﬁnite and compact Y ⊆ X contains the
discrete dense set Y \ {0}.
In the rest of the section, for arbitrary ultrametric space X and dense Y ⊆ X,
we introduce into consideration a “natural” bijection BX → BY and describe some
properties of this bijection.
Lemma 6.11. Let (X,d ) be a metric space, Y be a dense subset of X, and Z ⊆ X
be nonempty, bonded and open in X. Then the equality
(6.11) diamZ = diam(Z ∩ Y )
holds.
PAGE 47
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 47
Proof. It is clear that
(6.12) diamZ ⩾ diam(Z ∩ Y ).
Let ε> 0. Then there exist a, b ∈ Z and a∗, b∗ ∈ Z ∩ Y such that
diamZ ⩽d(a,b ) +ε
and
d(a,a ∗) ⩽ε, d (b,b ∗) ⩽ε.
Using the triangle inequality we obtain
|d(a,b ) − d(a∗,b ∗)|⩽d(a,a ∗) +d(b,b ∗) ⩽ 2ε.
Hence, the inequality
|diamZ − d(a∗,b ∗)|⩽ 3ε
holds. Consequently,
(6.13) diamZ ⩽d(a∗,b ∗) + 3ε ⩽ diam(Z ∩ Y ) + 3ε.
Since ε is an arbitrary positive number, (6.13) implies
diamZ ⩽ diam(Z ∩ Y ).
Now (6.11) follows from the last inequality and (6.12). □
The ballean BX of metric space (X,d ) can be considered as a poset with the
partial order ≼X generated by set inclusion
(6.14) (B1 ≼X B2) ⇔ (B1 ⊆ B2), B 1,B 2 ∈ BX.
Proposition 6.12. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty, ultrametric space and let Y be a dense
subset of X. Then the following statements hold:
(i) For every B ∈ BX, we have B ∩ Y ∈ BY .
(ii) The mapping Φ : BX → BY deﬁned by
(6.15) Φ(B) = B ∩ Y
is an order isomorphism of (BX, ≼X) and (BY, ≼Y ).
(iii) The equality
(6.16) diam Φ(B) = diam B
holds for every B ∈ BX.
Proof. (i) LetB ∈ BX and let r be the radius of B. Since B is a nonempty and open
subset ofX, the intersection B ∩Y is nonempty. Let y∗ ∈ B ∩Y . By Proposition 2.6,
we have
(6.17) B = {x ∈ X : d(x,y ∗)<r }.
The inclusion Y ⊆ X and equality (6.17) imply that
B ∩ Y = {y ∈ Y : d(y,y ∗)<r }.
Thus, B ∩ Y is an open ball in (Y,d |Y × Y ) and Φ, deﬁned by (6.15), is a mapping
from BX to BY .
(ii) By deﬁnition, for every B∗ ∈ BY there are c∗ ∈ Y and r∗ ∈ (0, ∞ ) such that
B∗ = {y ∈ Y : d(y,c ∗)<r ∗}.
Consequently,B∗ =Y ∩ {x ∈ X : d(x,c ∗)<r ∗} holds. Hence, Φ is surjective.
PAGE 48
48 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Now let B1 and B2 be diﬀerent elements of BX. Then we have
B1 \B2 ̸= ∅ or B2 \B1 ̸= ∅,
whereBi \Bj is the relative complement of Bj in Bi. Without loss of generality we
assume
(6.18) B1 \B2 ̸= ∅.
Proposition 2.5 implies that B1 is open andB2 is closed (inX). Consequently, B1\B2
is an open subset of X. Since Y is dense, the existence of a point y0 ∈ (B1 \B2) ∩Y
follows from (6.18). Consequently, we have
y0 ∈ B1 ∩ Y = Φ(B1) and y0 /∈ B2 ∩ Y = Φ(B2),
i.e., Φ(B1) ̸= Φ(B2). Hence, Φ : BX → BY is injective. Thus, Φ is a bijective
mapping.
It is clear that the mapping Φ is isotone. Since Φ is isotone and bijective, this
mapping is also strictly isotone and surjective. Moreover, it is clear that
(6.19) (B1 ∩ B2 = ∅) ⇒ (Φ(B1) ∩ Φ(B2) = ∅)
is valid for allB1,B2 ∈ BX. By Proposition 2.13, implication (6.19) can be rewriting
in the form
(B1 ∥X B2) ⇒ (Φ(B1) ∥Y Φ(B2)).
Now using Lemma 5.3 we see that Φ is an order isomorphism.
(iii) Equality (6.16) is a special case of equality (6.11) because everyB ∈ BX is
an open subset of X. □
If Z and W are isometric ultrametric spaces with an isometry Ψ :Z → W , then
the mapping
BZ ∋ B ↦→ Ψ(B) ∈ BW
is an order isomorphism of (BZ, ≼Z) and (BW, ≼W ). Consequently, Proposition 6.12
implies the following.
Theorem 6.13. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty, ultrametric spaces with com-
pletions ( ˜X, ˜d) and ( ˜Y, ˜ρ), respectively. If ( ˜X, ˜d) and ( ˜Y, ˜ρ) are isometric, then
(BX, ≼X) and (BY, ≼Y ) are order isomorphic.
It will be shown in the proof of Proposition 7.12 from the next section of the
paper that the mapping Φ : BX → BY , deﬁned by (6.15), is an isometry of BX and
BY if (X,d ) is a totally bounded ultrametric space and if we consider BX and BY
as metric spaces with corresponding Hausdorﬀ distances.
7. Metric properties of ultrametric balleans
In this section, we consider the sets of balls of completely b ounded, ultrametric
spaces (X,d ) together with the Hausdorﬀ distance dH on them. It is shown for
every compact, ultrametric space (X,d ) that the space (
BX,d H) is also a compact,
ultrametric space, and that the ballean BX of (X,d ) is dense (in BX ) and coincides
with the set of all isolated points of (BX,d H), Theorem 7.9. This result implies
that every totally bounded ultrametric space admits an isom etric embedding in a
compact, ultrametric space with the dense set of isolated po ints, Theorem 7.11.
Moreover, using the topological universality of Cantor set for the class of separable,
PAGE 49
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 49
ultrametric spaces, we show that every closed ball from the ﬁ eld of p-adic numbers
Qp with p = 2 is also topologically universal for this class, Theorem 7.1 8.
Let A and B be two nonempty, bounded subsets of a metric space (X,d ). The
Hausdorﬀ distance dH(A,B ) betweenA and B is deﬁned by
(7.1) dH(A,B ) := max
{
sup
a∈ A
inf
b∈ B
d(a,b ), sup
b∈ B
inf
a∈ A
d(a,b )
}
.
This deﬁnition and some properties of the Hausdorﬀ distance can be found in [23].
We note only that (7.1) implies
(7.2) dH({a}, {b}) = d(a,b )
for all a, b ∈ X.
Remark 7.1. The Hausdorﬀ distance was introduced by Felix Hausdorﬀ in 19 14 [66].
Later, Edwards [56] and, independently, Gromov [62] expand ed Hausdorﬀ construc-
tion to the class of all compact metric spaces. A modiﬁcation of Gromov—Hausdorﬀ
distance and Hausdorﬀ distance on ultrametrics were consid ered by Derong Qiu in
[105] and [106], respectively.
It is well-known that the Hausdorﬀ distance dH is a metric on the space of all
bounded, closed, nonempty subsets of arbitrary metric spac e (X,d ) (see, for exam-
ple, [23, Proposition 7.3.3]).
Using formula (7.2), we easily get the following.
Lemma 7.2. For every nonempty metric space (X,d ) the mapping
F : X →
BX, F (x) = {x},
is an isometric embedding of (X,d ) in (BX,d H).
It follows from Proposition 2.17 and the next lemma that the H ausdorﬀ distance
between any two distinct balls B1 andB2 of a compact, ultrametric space coincides
with the diameter of the smallest ball containing B1 ∪ B2.
Lemma 7.3. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty, ultrametric space. Then the equality
(7.3) dH(B1,B 2) = diam(B1 ∪ B2)
holds for all distinct balls B1, B2 ∈ BX.
Proof. LetB1 andB2 be distinct balls in (X,d ). If B1 ∩B2 = ∅ holds, then equality
(7.3) follows from (2.6) and (7.1). Suppose B1 ∩B2 ̸= ∅. Then, by Proposition 2.13,
we have either B1 ⊂ B2 or B2 ⊂ B1. Without loss of generality we assume
(7.4) B1 ⊂ B2.
If |B1|= 1, then there is p1 ∈ X such that
(7.5) B1 = {p1}.
Inclusion (7.4) implies that p1 ∈ B2. Using (2.2), (7.1) and (7.5) we obtain
dH(B1,B 2) = max
{
inf
p2∈ B2
d(p1,p 2), sup
p2∈ B2
d(p1,p 2)
}
= sup{d(p1,p 2) :p2 ∈ B2} = diamB2.
From (7.4) it follows that diamB2 = diam(B1 ∪ B2). Thus, (7.3) holds.

To complete the proof we must consider the case when
(7.6) B1 ⊂ B2
and diamB1 > 0. If (7.6) is true, then, in accordance with Lemma 2.1 [106],
(7.7) dH(B1,B 2) = dist(B1,B 2)
holds. Now (7.3) follows from (7.7) and (2.13) (see Remark 2. 14). □
The following lemma can be easily derived from Lemma 2.4 of [1 06].
Lemma 7.4. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty ultrametric space and let BX be the set of
all closed balls of (X,d ). Then the metric space (BX,d H) is ultrametric.
The following lemma is a reformulation of the classical Blas chke theorem.
Lemma 7.5. Let (X,d ) be a compact, nonempty metric space and let MX denote
the set of all nonempty, closed subsets of X. Then the metric space (MX,d H) is
also compact.
For the proof see, for example, [23, Theorem 7.3.8].
Lemma 7.6. Let (X,d ) be a compact, nonempty ultrametric space. Then the metric
space (BX,d H) is compact.
Proof. It is clear that BX ⊆ MX. By Lemma 7.5, the metric space (MX,d H) is
compact. Consequently, BX is totally bounded by Corollary 3.6. The Bolzano—
Weierstrass property implies that (BX,d H) is compact if every sequence (Bn)n∈ N ⊆
BX contains a convergent subsequence. From Proposition 3.5 it follows that every
(Bn)n∈ N ⊆ BX contains a Cauchy subsequence (Bnk)k∈ N. Using Lemma 7.3 we see
that if (Bnk)k∈ N is a Cauchy sequence, then either there are B ∈ BX and k0 ∈ N
such that
(7.8) Bnk =B
for all k ⩾k0, or the limit relation
(7.9) lim
n→∞
diamBnk = 0
holds. If we have (7.8) for all suﬃciently large k, then (Bnk)k∈ N is convergent and
lim
k→∞
Bnk =B.
Suppose (7.9) holds. Since (MX,d H) is compact and
BX ⊆ MX, the Cauchy se-
quence (Bnk)k∈ N is convergent to a set A ∈ MX,
(7.10) lim
k→∞
dH(A,B nk) = 0.
From (7.10) and (7.1) it follows that
(7.11) lim
k→∞
sup
x∈ A
inf
y∈ Bnk
d(x,y ) = 0.
Using limit relations (7.9) and (7.11) it is easy to prove tha t there is a ∈ X for
which {a} =A holds. To complete the proof it suﬃces to note that {a} ∈
BX. □
Lemma 7.7. Let (X,d ) be a compact, nonempty ultrametric space. Then the ballean
BX of (X,d ) coincides with the set of all isolated points of the ultramet ric space
(BX,d H).

Proof. By Corollary 6.2, we have the inclusion
(7.12) BX ⊆ BX.
We ﬁrst show that every B∗ ∈ BX is an isolated point of (BX,d H). If B∗ ∈ BX and
diamB∗ > 0, then, by Lemma 7.3, we obtain
dH(B∗,B ) = diam(B∗ ∪ B) ⩾ diamB∗ > 0
for every B ∈ BX. Thus, B∗ is an isolated point in (BX,d H).
Let us consider now the case when B∗ ∈ BX and diamB∗ = 0. In this case there
isc∗ ∈ X such that B∗ = {c∗}. Since B∗ is a clopen subset of (X,d ), the point c∗ is
an isolated point of (X,d ) (by Proposition 2.5). Consequently, there is ε∗ > 0 such
that
(7.13) d(c∗,x )>ε ∗ > 0
for every x ∈ X \ {c∗}. Double inequality (7.13) and deﬁnition (7.1) imply

wheneverB ∈ BX and B ̸=B∗.

(7.14) B∗ ∈
BX \BX
is an accumulation point of (BX,d H).
Suppose (7.14) holds. Then, by Lemma 6.4, we obtain
B∗ = {c∗}
for a point c∗ ∈ X and, in addition, the point c∗ is an accumulation point of X, by
Proposition 2.5. Hence, for every ε> 0, the ball Bε(c∗) ∈ BX satisﬁes the inequality
dH(B∗,B ε(c∗)) ⩽ε
and the condition B∗ ̸=Bε(c∗). Thus, B∗ is an accumulation point in (BX,d H). □
Analyzing the proof of Lemma 7.7 we also obtain the following .
Lemma 7.8. Let (X,d ) be a compact, nonempty ultrametric space. Then the set of
all isolated points of (BX,d H) is a dense subset of BX.
Putting together all lemmas proved above, we obtain the foll owing result.
Theorem 7.9. Let (X,d ) be a compact, nonempty ultrametric space and let (BX,d H)
be the space of all closed balls of (X,d ) with the Hausdorﬀ metric dH. Then (BX,d H)
is also a compact, ultrametric space for which the ballean BX ⊆ BX coincides with
the set of all isolated points of (BX,d H) and this set is dense in BX .
Remark 7.10. Using Proposition 2.13 (see, in particular, formulas (2.6) and (2.13))
one can easily proves that, for ultrametric space (X,d ), a sequence (Bi)i∈ N ⊆ BX is
convergent in (BX,d H) if and only if (Bi)i∈ N is convergent in the so-called Wijsman
topology, which was ﬁrstly studied by Lechicki and Levi [84] . It is interesting to
note that, for every metric space (Y,ρ ), a sequence of subsets of Y is convergent
with respect to the Vietoris topology generated by ρ if and only if this sequence
is Wijsman convergent for all metrics which are topological ly equivalent to ρ [7].
See also [11] for characterization of Wijsman convergence v ia Wijsman statistical
convergence by moduli.

Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be metric spaces. An injective mapping Φ :X → Y is called
an isometric embedding of (X,d ) in (Y,ρ ) if the equality
d(x,y ) = ρ(Φ(x), Φ(y))
holds for all x, y ∈ X. In this case we write (X,d )֒→( Y,ρ ) and say that (X,d )
admits an isometric embedding in (Y,ρ ).
Theorem 7.11. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space. Then there is
a compact ultrametric space (Y,ρ ) such that the set of isolated points of (Y,ρ ) is a
dense subset of Y and (X,d ) admits an isometric embedding (X,d )֒→( Y,ρ ).

( ˜X, ˜d) that there is an isometric embedding
(X,d )
Φ
− → ( ˜X, ˜d).
By Lemma 7.2, the mapping
F : ˜X → BX, F (x) = {x}
is also an isometric embedding. Consequently,
X
