− → ˜X
F
− → BX
is an isometric embedding of (X,d ) in (BX,d H). The metric space (BX,d H) is
compact and ultrametric with the dense set of isolated point s (by Theorem 7.9). □
Theorem 7.11 has some extensions for subspaces (X,d ) of ﬁnite dimensional Eu-

Proposition 7.12. Let (X,d ) be a totally bounded ultrametric space and let ( ˜X, ˜d)
be the completion of (X,d ). Then the metric spaces (B ˜X, ˜dH) and (BX,d H) are

Proof. It is clear that, if (X1,d 1) and (X2,d 2) are isometric ultrametric spaces,

Corollary 7.13. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be totally bounded ultrametric spaces. If the
completion ( ˜X, ˜d) and ( ˜Y, ˜ρ) are isometric, then the ultrametric spaces (BX,d H)
and (BY,ρ H ) are isometric.
Let F be a family of metric spaces. A metric space (Y,ρ ) is metrically (topo-
logically) universal for F if every (X,d ) ∈ F admits an isometric (topological)
embedding (X,d )֒→( Y,ρ ).
Theorem 7.11 gives rise the following interesting question .
Problem 7.14. Let F be a family of totally bounded ultrametric spaces. Under
what condition there exists a totally bounded ultrametric s pace which is metrically
universal for F ?
The following proposition and Theorem 7.16 below show that t he sets of isolated

spaces are small from set-theoretic point of view.

r we see that (7.16) implies
|F∗
r |< ∞ . The inequality |BX(r)|< ∞ follows from |F∗
r |< ∞ and (7.17). □
In the rest of the section we will discuss some questions rela ted to topologically
universal, compact, ultrametric spaces and metrically uni versal ones.
Remark 7.17. The universal spaces for the class S of all separable metric spaces
are studied in some details. The investigations in this dire ction were initiated by
P. S. Urysohn for topologically S-universal spaces in [123] and in [122, 124] for met-
rically S-universal ones. The space C([0, 1]) of all continuous, real-valued functions
with the sup-norm is separable and metrically S-universal by famous Banach theo-
rem [5]. Metrically universal, separable, metric spaces ar e constructed S. Iliadis [72]
for classes of separable metric spaces having some restrict ions on dimension of these
spaces. (See also [73–75, 91, 92, 99, 119, 131].) The class SU of all ultrametric,
separable spaces is an important subclass of S. It was proved by A. F. Timan
and I. A. Vestfrid [121] that the space l2 of real sequences (xn)n∈ N with the norm(∑
n∈ Nx2
n
) 1/2
is metrically SU-universal and it is clear that l2 is not metrically
S-universal. The ﬁrst example of metrically SU-universal ultrametric space was
PAGE 55
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 55
constructed by I. A. Vestfrid [129]. As was proved by A. Lemin and V. Lemin in
[89], if an ultrametric space (Y,ρ ) is metrically universal for all two-points spaces,
then the weight of (Y,ρ ) is not less than the continuum c. Consequently, no SU-
universal, ultrametric space can be separable. In this conn ection it should be pointed
out that, under some set-theoretic assumptions, for every c ardinal τ > c there is
an ultrametric space (X,d ) of the weight τ such that every ultrametric space with
the weight τ can be isometrically embedded into (X,d ). The last statement was
proved by J. Vaughan [127]. Some results related to “minimal ”, metrically universal,
ultrametric spaces can be found in [13].
The following theorem implies that every nonempty open set i n the ﬁeld Q2 is
topologically universal for the class SU of all separable, ultrametric spaces.
Theorem 7.18. Let (Q2,d 2) be the ultrametric space of 2-adic numbers, d2(x,y ) =
|x − y|2. Then every B ∈ BQ2 is topologically universal for the class SU of all
separable, ultrametric spaces.
Proof. Theorem 6.2.16 [57] implies that the Cantor set Dℵ 0 is a topologically uni-
versal space for SU. It was shown in Example 3.13 that Dℵ 0 is homeomorphic the
closed unit ball
B1(0) of ultrametric space (Q2,d 2). From Theorem 51 [113] it follows
that every bounded subset of (Qp,d p) is totally bounded. Hence, by Lemma 6.4,
we obtain B1(0) ∈ BQ2. For every nonzero a ∈ Q2 and every b ∈ Q2 the function
F : Q2 → Q2 deﬁned as
(7.18) F (x) = ax +b
is simultaneously a linear bijection on the ﬁeld Q2 and a self-homeomorphism of
the ultrametric space (Q2,d 2). The distance set D(Q2,d 2) of the ultrametric space
(Q2,d 2) is
{2j : j ∈ Z} ∪ { 0}.
Hence, the inclusion
(7.19) D(Q2,d 2) ⊆ Q2
holds. Now let B ∈ BQ2 be given and let b ∈ B. Write a = diamB and consider F
deﬁned by (7.18). From b ∈ B ⊆ Q2 and (7.19) it follows that F is correctly deﬁned
andF (
B1(0)) = B holds. The restriction F |B1(0) is a homeomorphism of B1(0) and
B. Thus, B is topologically universal for SU as required. □
Remark 7.19. Isometrical embeddings of ultrametric spaces in non-Archi medean
valued ﬁeld were constructed by W. H. Schikhof [112].
It can be proved that a metric space (X,d ) is separable if and only if there is a
metricρ such thatd andρ are topologically equivalent and (X,ρ ) is totally bounded.
(It is a reformulation of Theorem 4.3.5 [57].) Theorem 7.18 g ives us the following
reﬁnement of this result for ultrametric spaces.
Corollary 7.20. Let (X,d ) be an ultrametric space. Then (X,d ) is separable if and
only if there is an ultrametric ρ : X × X → R+ such that (X,ρ ) is totally bounded
and ρ is topologically equivalent to d.
Corollary 7.20 and Theorem 5.19 imply that, for every inﬁnit e separable ultra-
metric space (X,d ), there is an ultrametric ρ : X × X → R+ such that d and ρ
PAGE 56
56 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
are topologically equivalent, 0 is an unique accumulation point of D(X,ρ ), and the
(ordered) distance set
D = {ρ(x,y ) :x,y ∈ X}
has the order type 1 + ω∗.
8. Free representing trees
The main goal of the section is to introduce into considerati on a (free) representing
treeTX for every nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ) and prove some
properties of TX . Using concept of diametrical graph, we inductively constr uct TX
for every nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ) (see Deﬁnition 8.1
below). In Proposition 8.9 it is proved that TX is a locally ﬁnite tree for which the
equalityV (TX ) = BX holds. The structure of representing trees TX corresponding
to ultrametric compacts which are homeomorphic to the Canto r set is described in
Proposition 8.10.
Deﬁnition 8.1. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ).
Then we associate with (X,d ) a graph TX by the following rule:
(i1) V (TX ) is a subset of 2X = {A : A ⊆ X}.
(i2) The set X is a vertex of TX.
(i3) A set A ⊂ X belongs to V (TX) if and only if there exists a ﬁnite sequence
X1, ... , Xn of subsets of X such that X1 = X, Xn = A and, for every
i ∈ { 1,...,n − 1}, Xi+1 is a part of GXi,d|Xi× Xi
.
(i4) The verticesA,B ∈ V (TX ) are adjacent if and only if A is a part of GB,d|B× B
or B is a part of GA,d|A× A.
Remark 8.2. Let A be a subset of totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ). Using
Example 4.9, Theorem 4.10 and Corollary 4.12 it is easy to pro ve that the diametrical
graph GA,d|A× A is complete multipartite if and only if |A| ⩾ 2 holds. Now in the
correspondence with condition (i3) of Deﬁnition 8.1, we see that if B ∈ V (TX ) and
B ̸= X, then there is A ∈ V (TX) such that B is a part of GA,d|A× A. Consequently,
everyB ∈ V (TX ) is a nonempty subset of X. Moreover, condition (i4) implies that
the inequality
max{|A|, |B|}⩾ 2
holds whenever {A,B } ∈ E(TX ).
To study the properties of TX, it is convenient to rank the set V (TX ) by labeling
V (TX) ∋ A ↦→ lev(A) ∈ N ∪ { 0}
deﬁned as lev(X) = 0 and if A ∈ V (TX ) and A ̸=X, then
(8.1) lev(A) = n − 1,
where n is the smallest positive integer satisfying condition (i3) of Deﬁnition 8.1.
We will say that A is a vertex on the level n − 1 (or A has the level n − 1) if (8.1)
holds.

Lemma 8.4. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space. Then the
equality
V (TX ) = BX
holds.
Proof. This lemma is trivial if |X|= 1. Suppose that |X|⩾ 2. At ﬁrst we prove the
inclusion V (TX ) ⊆ BX.
It is clear that X ∈ BX. Let GX,d be the diametrical graph of (X,d ). Then,
by Theorem 4.10 and Proposition 4.11, there is an integer k ⩾ 2 such that GX,d
is complete k-partite with some parts X1, ... , Xk ∈ BX. Since X is the unique
subset of X on the zero level, a set A ⊆ X belongs to V (TX) and has the level 1,
lev(A) = 1 , if and only if A =Xi for some i ∈ { 1,...,k }.
Let us deﬁne a subset F1 of {X1,...,X n} as
F1 := {Xi : |Xi|⩾ 2 and 1 ⩽i ⩽k}.
If F1 = ∅, then V (TX ) = {X} ∪ {X1,...,X n} and the inclusion V (TX) ⊆ BX fol-
lows from Theorem 4.10. Let F1 ̸= ∅ andX ∗ ∈ F 1. Then (X ∗,d |X ∗ × X ∗ ) is a totally
bounded ultrametric space by Proposition 3.5. Consequentl y, by Proposition 4.11,
the diametrical graph GX ∗ ,d|X∗ × X∗ is complete multipartite and, moreover, by The-
orem 4.10, every part of GX ∗ ,d|X∗ × X∗ is an open ball in the space (X ∗,d |X ∗ × X ∗ ). By
Corollary 2.7, we have the inclusion
BX ∗ ⊆ BX.
Consequently, if A ∈ V (TX ) and lev(A) = 2 , then the membership
(8.2) A ∈ BX
is valid. Repeating the above reasons for X ∗ ∈ V (TX) with diamX ∗ > 0 and
lev(X ∗) = n, n = 2, 3, ... we obtain (8.2) for all A ∈ V (TX ).
Now the desirable inclusion
(8.3) V (TX ) ⊆ BX
follows from Deﬁnition 8.1.
PAGE 58
58 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Let us prove the converse inclusion
(8.4) BX ⊆ V (TX).
Suppose BX \V (TX) ̸= ∅ and let B1 belong to BX \V (TX). By Corollary 6.5,
there is B2 ∈ BX such that B1 is a part of GB2,d|B2× B2
and diamB2 > 0 (see
Remark 6.6). If B2 ∈ V (TX ), then we also have B1 ∈ V (TX) by Deﬁnition 8.1.
Thus, B2 ∈ BX \V (TX) holds. Consequently, there is B3 ∈ BX such that B2 is a
part of GB3,d|B3× B3
. Since B1 ⊆ B2 ⊆ B3, the double inequality
(8.5) diamB1 ⩽ diamB2 ⩽ diamB3
holds. By Corollary 6.2, we have BX ⊆ BX . Using (8.5), Lemma 2.9 and the
relation B1 ̸=B2 ̸=B3, we obtain
diamB1 < diamB2 < diamB3.
Repeating this construction, we can ﬁnd a sequence
(Bi)i∈ N ⊆ BX
such that diamBi < diamBi+1 holds for every i ∈ N. Consequently, the distance set
D contains a strictly increasing, inﬁnite sequence, contrar y to Theorem 5.19. □
Lemma 8.5. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space with the
distance set D = D(X) and let B1, B2 ∈ BX be diﬀerent. Then the following
statements are equivalent:
(i) {B1,B 2} ∈ E(TX ) is valid.
(ii) There are r ∈ D \ {0} and c ∈ B1 ∩ B2 such that
diamB1 =r and B2 = {x ∈ X : d(x,c )<r }
or
diamB2 =r and B1 = {x ∈ X : d(x,c )<r }.
(iii) For every B ∈ BX we have either
(B1 ⊂ B2) and ((B1 ⊆ B ⊆ B2) ⇒ (B1 =B or B2 =B))
or
(B2 ⊂ B1) and ((B2 ⊆ B ⊆ B1) ⇒ (B1 =B or B2 =B)).
Proof. Let us prove the validity of (i) ⇔ (ii). First of all we note that V (TX ) = BX
holds by Lemma 8.4. Using condition (i4) from the deﬁnition of TX we see that the
equivalence (i) ⇔ (ii) is valid by Theorem 4.10 if B1 = X or B2 = X. If B1 and
B2 are arbitrary open balls in (X,d ), then to prove the validity of (i) ⇔ (ii) we can
use Theorem 4.10 together with Corollary 2.7.
(ii) ⇒ (iii). Suppose (ii) holds.
Let B1 ⊂ B2, B ∈ BX and
(8.6) B1 ⊆ B ⊆ B2.
We must show that B1 =B or B2 =B. Suppose B1 ̸=B. Using (ii) we obtain
B1 = {x ∈ X : d(x,c )<r } and diamB2 =r.
Consequently, from B1 ̸=B and B1 ⊆ B it follows that d(c,p 1) ⩾r. Hence
(8.7) diamB ⩾r = diamB2.
PAGE 59
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 59
Now using Proposition 2.10 (recall that BX ⊆ BX holds by Corollary 6.2), the
inclusion B ⊆ B2 and (8.7) we obtain the equality B2 =B.
The case B2 ⊂ B1 is similar. Thus, the implication (ii) ⇒ (iii) is true.
(iii) ⇒ (i). Let (iii) hold and let B2 ⊂ B1. It suﬃces to show that B2 is a part
of the complete multipartite graph GB1,d|B1× B1
. Let {X1,...,X n} be the set of all
parts of this graph. If there are diﬀerent n1, n2 ∈ { 1,...,n } such that
B2 ∩ Xn1 ̸= ∅ ̸=B2 ∩ Xn2,
then we have diamB2 = diamB1 and, consequently, B1 = B2 by Proposition 2.10,
contrary to B2 ⊂ B1. Hence, there is n∗ ∈ { 1,...,n } such that B2 ⊆ Xn∗ . If
B2 ̸= Xn∗ , then using (iii) with B = Xn∗ we obtain the equality Xn∗ = B1, that
contradicts the deﬁnition of GB1,d|B1× B1
. Thus, B2 =Xn∗ holds, as required. □
Lemma 8.6. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space and let
B1, B2, B3 ∈ BX. If B1 is a part of GBi,d|Bi× Bi
for i = 2, 3, then B2 =B3 holds.
Proof. Let B1 be a part of GB2,d|B2× B2
and, simultaneously, a part of GB3,d|B3× B3
.
Since B1 ⊆ B2, and B1 ⊆ B3, and B1 ̸= ∅, we have B2 ⊆ B3 or B3 ⊆ B2 by
Proposition 2.13. Without loss of generality we assume B2 ⊆ B3. Then we have
{B1,B 3} ∈ E(TX) and B1 ⊆ B2 ⊆ B3. Now B2 =B3 follows from Lemma 8.5. □
Let v be a vertex of a graph G and let k be a cardinal number. The vertex
v ∈ V (G) has the degreek if
k = card{u ∈ V (G) : {u,v } ∈ E(G)}.
In this case we write δG(v) = k or simply δ(v) = k.
The next lemma follows directly from the deﬁnition of TX .
Lemma 8.7. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space. Then the
following equality
(8.8) δ(B) =









0 if B =X and diamX = 0,
k if B =X and diamX >0,
1 +k if diamB >0 and B ̸=X,
1 if diamB = 0 and B ̸=X,
holds for every B ∈ V (TX ), where k is the number of parts of complete multipartite
graphGB,d|B× B .
Recall that a graph C is a subgraph of a graph G, C ⊆ G, if
V (C) ⊆ V (G) and E(C) ⊆ E(G).
A path is a ﬁnite, nonempty graph P whose vertices can be numbered so that
V (P ) = {x0,x 1,...,x k}, k ⩾ 1, and E(P ) = {{x0,x 1},..., {xk− 1,x k}}.
In this case we say that P is a path joining x0 and xk.
A graph G is connected if for every two distinct u, v ∈ V (G) there is a path
P ⊆ G joining u and v.
Deﬁnition 8.8. A connected graph without cycles is called a tree. A tree T is
locally ﬁnite if the inequality δ(v)< ∞ holds for every v ∈ V (T ).
We will say that a vertex v of a tree T is a leaf of T if the inequality δ(v) ⩽ 1
holds.

Proposition 8.9. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space. Then
TX is a locally ﬁnite tree such that V (TX ) = BX and the equation
(8.9) δ(v) = 2
has at most one solution v ∈ V (TX ).
Proof. First of all, V (TX) = BX holds by Lemma 8.4. We claim that TX is a tree.
Suppose contrary that there is a cycle C ⊆ TX . Let
V (C) = {B1,...,B n} and E(C) = {{B1,B 2},..., {Bn− 1,B n}, {Bn,B 1}}.
After renumbering, if necessary, we can assume
diamB1 ⩾ diamB
for every B ∈ V (C). If we have diamB1 = diamB2, then the equality
(8.10) B1 =B2
holds. Indeed, we have V (TX ) = BX and, by Corollary 6.2, the inclusion BX ⊆
BX
holds. Consequently, the conditions
B1, B2 ∈ BX, diamB1 = diamB2 and B1 ∩ B2 ̸= ∅
are satisﬁed. Now using Proposition 2.10 we obtain (8.10). T hus, diamB1 >
diamB2 holds. Similarly, we obtain the inequality
(8.11) diamB1 > diamBn.
Inequality diamB1 > diamB2 and {B1,B 2} ∈ E(TX ) imply that B2 is a part of
GB1,d|B1× B1
. From {B2,B 3} ∈ E(TX ) and the deﬁnition of E(TX ) it follows that
(8.12) B2 is a part of GB3,d|B3× B3
or
(8.13) B3 is a part of GB2,d|B2× B2
.
If (8.12) holds, then, by Lemma 8.6, we have B3 =B1, that contradicts the deﬁnition
of graphs. Similarly, we can show that Bi+1 is a part of GBi,d|Bi× Bi
for i = 3 , ... , n
and, in addition,
(8.14) B1 is a part of GBn,d|Bn× Bn.
From (8.14) it follows that diamBn > diamB1, contrary to (8.11). Thus, TX does
not contain any cycle.
Now we claim that TX is a connected graph. Indeed, let B belong to V (TX) and
lev(B) = n, n ∈ N. Then, by condition (i3) of Deﬁnition 8.1, there is B1 ∈ V (TX)
such that lev(B1) = n − 1 and B is a part of GB1,d|B1× B1
. Hence, {B,B 1} ∈ E(TX )
by condition (i4). If n − 1 ̸= 0, then completely similarly we can ﬁnd B2 ∈ V (TX ),
lev(B2) = n − 2 and {B1,B 2} ∈ E(TX) and so on. Then the graph P ⊆ TX with
V (P ) = {B,B 1,...,B n} and E(P ) = {{B,B 1}, {B1,B 2},..., {Bn− 1,B n}}
is a path joining B andBn. It is clear that P ⊆ TX . Moreover, we have the equalities
lev(B) = n, lev(B1) = n − 1, lev(Bj) = n − j,..., lev(Bn) = n − n = 0.
Consequently, by Deﬁnition 8.1, the equality Bn = X holds. Thus, for every B ∈
V (TX ), there is a path joining B andX inTX . Hence, TX is connected and acyclic.
Thus, it is a tree.

TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 61
Using Deﬁnition 8.1, Proposition 4.1 and Lemma 8.6 we see tha t TX is locally
ﬁnite.
To complete the proof it suﬃces to note that if (8.9) holds for v ∈ V (TX ), then
v =X by Lemma 8.7. □
In what follows we will say that TX is a free representing tree of (X,d ).
As an application of Proposition 8.9, we consider a characte rization of the Cantor
setDℵ 0 (see Example 3.13) on the language of free representing tree s. The following
proposition is, in fact, a partial reformulation of the clas sical Brouwer’s result [22].
Proposition 8.10. Let (X,d ) be a compact ultrametric space. Then the following
statements are equivalent:
(i) (X,d ) is homeomorphic to the Cantor set Dℵ 0.
(ii) The representing tree TX contains no leaves.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Let (X,d ) and Dℵ 0 be homeomorphic. By deﬁnition, a vertex
v ∈ V (TX ) is a leaf of TX if and only if the inequality δ(v) ⩽ 1 holds. Since (X,d )
is homeomorphic to Dℵ 0, we have the inequality diamX > 0. The last inequality,
the equality BX = V (TX), and Lemma 8.7 imply that δ(v) ⩾ 1 holds for every
v ∈ V (TX). Consequently, if v0 is a leaf of TX, then the equality
(8.15) δ(v0) = 1
holds. Using Lemma 8.7 again we see (8.15) implies the existe nce of an open ball
B0 with diamB0 = 0 , i.e., we have B0 = {p0} for a point p0 ∈ X. Hence, p0 is
an isolated point of X. Since (X,d ) and Dℵ 0 are homeomorphic, the existence of
isolated p0 ∈ X implies the existence of an isolated point in Dℵ 0, contrary to the
deﬁnition of Dℵ 0.
(ii) ⇒ (i). Suppose that TX contains no leaves. By Brouwer theorem, every zero-
dimensional compact metric space without isolated points i s homeomorphic to the
Cantor set [22], [57, p. 370, Exercise 6.2.A]. A nonempty met ric space is called zero-
dimensional if Y has a base consisting of clopen sets. Since the ballean of a me tric
space is a base for this space, every nonempty ultrametric sp ace is zero-dimensional
by Proposition 2.5. Consequently, it suﬃces to show that (X,d ) has no isolated
points. To prove the last statement we note that if p0 is an isolated point of (X,d ),
thenB0 = {p0} ∈ BX holds and, consequently, δ(B0) ⩽ 1 holds by Lemma 8.7. The
last inequality and deﬁnition of the leaves imply that B0 is a leaf of TX, contrary to
supposition. □
9. Rooted trees and related orderings
The present section is organized as follows. The ﬁrst part of section deals with
partial order generated by choice of a root on trees. The orde rings of such type
are characterized in Theorem 9.9. In the second part, we cons ider the rooted rep-
resenting trees of totally bounded ultrametric spaces and d escribe them in Proposi-
tion 9.18.
9.1. Generating order by choosing root of the tree
Before introducing into consideration the concept of isomo rphism of rooted trees,
it is useful to recall the deﬁnition of isomorphism for free t rees.
PAGE 62
62 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Deﬁnition 9.1. Let T1 and T2 be trees. A bijection f : V (T1) → V (T2) is an
isomorphism of T1 and T2 if
(9.1) ({u,v } ∈ E(T1)) ⇔ ({f (u),f (v)} ∈ E(T2))
is valid for all u,v ∈ V (T1). Two trees are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism
of these trees.
A rooted tree is a pair (T,r ), where T is a tree and r is a distinguished vertex of
T . In this case we say that r is the root of (T,r ) and T is a free tree corresponding
to (T,r ). In what follows, we will write T =T (r) instead of (T,r ).
Deﬁnition 9.2. Let T1 = T1(r1) and T2 = T2(r2) be rooted trees. A bijection
f : V (T1) → V (T2) is an isomorphism of T1(r1) and T2(r2) if it is an isomorphism
of free trees T1 and T1 and the equality f (r1) = r2 holds. Two rooted trees are
isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism of these trees.
Remark 9.3. In the case of ﬁnite trees, Deﬁnitions 9.1 and 9.2 introduced above as
well as Deﬁnition 10.15 from the next section of the paper can be considered as some
speciﬁcations of Deﬁnition 1 from [70].
The following statement is well-known for ﬁnite trees (see, for example, Proposi-
tion 4.1 [16]).
Lemma 9.4. In each tree, every two diﬀerent vertices are connected by ex actly one
path.
Proof. IfT is an inﬁnite tree and v1, v2 are two diﬀerent vertices of T connected by
some paths P1 ⊆ T and P2 ⊆ T , then the graph G,
V (G) = V (P1) ∪ V (P2) and E(G) = E(P1) ∪ E(P2),
is a ﬁnite, connected subgraph of T . Since T does not have cycles, G is also acyclic.
Hence, G is a ﬁnite tree and P1, P2 are paths connected v1 and v2 in G. Thus,
P1 =P2 holds. □
Corollary 9.5. Let T be a tree and let P1 and P2 be some paths in T . Then the
equalityP1 =P2 holds if and only if V (P1) = V (P2).
Let introduce a partial order generated by choice of a root in a given tree.
Let T (r) be a rooted tree and let P : V (T (r)) → 2V (T (r)) be a mapping such that
(9.2) P(v) =
{
V (Pv) if v ̸=r,
{r} if v =r,
where Pv is the unique path connected v and r. Then we deﬁne a binary relation
≼T (r) on V (T (r)) by the rule
(9.3) (v1 ≼T (r) v2) ⇔ (P(v2) ⊆ P (v1)).
Proposition 9.6. The binary relation ≼T (r), deﬁned by (9.2), (9.3), is a partial
order on V (T (r)) for every rooted tree T (r).
Proof. Let T = T (r) be a rooted tree. It is clear from (9.2) and (9.3) that ≼T (r)
is reﬂexive and transitive. To justify the antisymmetric pr operty suppose that we
have
(9.4) v1 ≼T (r) v2 and v2 ≼T (r) v1.
PAGE 63
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 63
for some v1, v2 ∈ V (T ) and prove the equality v1 =v2.
From (9.2) and (9.4) it follows that P(v1) = P(v2). If |P(v1)|= |P(v2)|= 1, then
v1 =v2 =r holds by (9.2). If P(v1) and P(v2) contain more than one point, then,
by (9.2), we have v1 ̸=r ̸=v2 and, consequently, there is a path Pvi ⊆ T connected
r with vi, i = 1 , 2. By Corollary 9.5, it follows that Pv1 = Pv2. Now the equality
v1 =v2 follows from the deﬁnition of paths. □
We want to describe the posets (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)) up to (order) isomorphism. For
this description the concepts of covering relation and tran sitive closure of binary
relation shall be used.
Let us deﬁne the covering relation on a given partially order ed set.
Deﬁnition 9.7 ([114, p. 6]) . Let (P, ≼P ) be a poset and let x, y ∈ P . Then x is
called a lower cover of y (and y is called an upper cover of x) if x ≺ P y and
(9.5) (x ≼P z ≼P y) ⇒ (z =x or z =y)
is valid for every z ∈ P . If x is a lower cover of y, we write x ❁ P y (y ❂ P x).
Moreover, we use the symbol x ⊑ P y (y ⊒ P x) if x ❁ P y or x =y.
Let X be a set and let R1 and R2 be binary relations on X, i.e., R1 and R2
are some subsets of the Cartesian square X 2 = {⟨x,y ⟩ : x,y ∈ X}. Recall that a
composition of binary relations R1 and R2 is a binary relation R1 ◦R2 ⊆ X 2 for
which ⟨x,y ⟩ ∈ R1 ◦R2 holds if and only if there is z ∈ X such that ⟨x,z ⟩ ∈ R1 and
⟨z,y ⟩ ∈ R2.
Using the composition of binary relations we can deﬁne the tr ansitive closure of
a relation as follows.
Letγ be a binary relation on X. We will write γ1 =γ andγn+1 =γn ◦γ for every
integern ⩾ 1. The transitive closure γt of γ is the relation
(9.6) γt =
∞⋃
n=1
γn.
For every β ⊆ X 2, the transitive closure β t is a transitive binary relation on X and
the inclusion β ⊆ β t holds. Moreover, if τ ⊆ X 2 is an arbitrary transitive binary
relation for which β ⊆ τ, then we also have β t ⊆ τ, i.e., β t is the smallest transitive
binary relation containing β .
It is easy to see that, for every poset (P, ≼P ), the relation ⊑ t
P is a partial order
on P satisfying the inclusion
⊑ t
P ⊆ ≼P,
where ⊑ t
P is the transitive closure of ⊑ P .
For the ﬁnite posets, it is well-known that the upper coverin g relation uniquely
determines the partial order: If (P, ≼P ) is a ﬁnite poset, then the equality
(9.7) ≼P = ⊑ t
P
holds.
It should be noted here that (9.7) can be fails in general.
Example 9.8. Let (R, ⩽R) be the totally ordered set of all real numbers with the
standard order ⩽R. Then the transitive closure ⊑ t
R coincides with the diagonal
relation on R,
⊑ t
R = {⟨x,x ⟩ : x ∈ R}.

Theorem 9.9. Let (S, ≼S) be a poset. Then following conditions (i) and (ii) are
equivalent.
(i) There is a rooted tree T (r) such that (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)) and (S, ≼S) are order
isomorphic.
(ii) The poset (S, ≼S) has the following properties:
(ii1) S contains the largest element l.
(ii2) If p ∈ S is not the largest element of S, then p admits a unique
upper cover q, i.e., there is the unique q ∈ S such that p ❁ S q.
(ii3) The equality ≼S = ⊑ t
S holds.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). To justify this implication it suﬃces to show that, for ever y
rooted tree T (r), the poset (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)) has properties (ii1), (ii2) and (ii3) with
S =V (T (r)) and ≼S = ≼T (r).
(ii1). Let T = T (r) be a rooted tree and let ≼T (r) be deﬁned by (9.3). Then,
for every v ∈ V (T ) \ {r}, it is clear that the inclusion {r} ⊆ V (Pv) holds, where
Pv ⊆ T is a path joining v with the root r. Hence, r is the largest element of
(V (T (r)), ≼T (r)).
(ii2). Let v0 ∈ V (T ) \ {r}. Then there is a unique path Pv0 ⊆ T such that
(9.8) V (Pv0) = {v0,v 1,...,v k}, k ⩾ 1, v k =r
and
(9.9) E(Pv0) =
{

}
.
It is clear from (9.8), (9.9) that
(9.10) V (Pv1) = {v1,...,v k},
where Pv1 ⊆ T is the path joining v1 with r. Equalities (9.8), (9.10) and the
deﬁnition of ≼T (r) imply v0 ≺ T (r) v1.
To prove the validity of
(9.11) v0 ❁ T (r) v1
it suﬃces to show that if u ∈ V (T ) and
(9.12) v0 ≼T (r) u ≼T (r) v1
holds, then we have
(9.13) u =v0 or u =v1
(see Deﬁnition 9.7). By (9.2), double inequality (9.12) can be written as
{v1,...,v k} ⊆ V (Pu) ⊆ { v0,v 1,...,v k},
that implies
(9.14) V (Pu) = {v1,...,v k} or V (Pu) = {v0,v 1,...,v k},
whereV (Pu) is the path joining u withr inT . Now (9.13) follows from (9.8), (9.10),
(9.14).
(ii3). Let us prove the equality
(9.15) ≼T (r) = ⊑ t
T (r).

(ii) ⇒ (i). Let a partially ordered set (S, ≼S) have properties (ii1), (ii2) and (ii3).
Let us deﬁne a rooted graph T = T (r) such that V (T ) = S, r = l, where l is the

(9.19) ({u,v } ∈ E(T )) ⇔ (u ❁ S v or v ❁ S u)

Lemma 9.11. Let T = T (r) be a rooted tree. Let us denote by ❁ T (r) the upper
covering relation corresponding to the partial order ≼T (r). Then the equivalence
(9.29) (v ❁ T (r) u or u ❁ T (r) v) ⇔ ({u,v } ∈ E(T ))
is valid for all u, v ∈ V (T ).
Proof. Equivalence (9.29) is evidently valid if |V (T )|= 1. Suppose |V (T )|⩾ 2. Let
u andv be vertices of T such that v ❁ T (r) u. The root r is the largest element of the
poset (V (T ), ≼T (r)). Hence, v ❁ T (r) u implies v ̸= r. By Lemma 9.4, there is the
unique path Pv = (v1,...,v n) ⊆ T with v1 = v and vn = r. Let w be an arbitrary
vertex ofT . By deﬁnition of ≼T (r), the inequality v ≼T (r) w holds if and only if there
is i ∈ { 1,...,n } for which we have w = vi. Consequently, there is i0 ∈ { 1,...,n }
such that vi0 =u. It is clear that i0 ⩾ 2. If the equality i0 = 2 holds, then
{v,u } = {v1,v i0} ∈ E(Pv).
Suppose that i0 ⩾ 3. Then v2 ̸=u and we get
v ≼T (r) v2 ≼T (r) vi0 =u.
It means that v ❁ T (r) v2 and, consequently, u is not the upper cover of v, contrary
to v ❁ T (r) u. The implication
(v ❁ T (r) u) ⇒ ({u,v } ∈ E(T ))
follows. Since T is a simple graph, we have
({u,v } ∈ E(T )) ⇔ ({v,u } ∈ E(T )).
Thus, the implication
(v ❁ T (r) u or u ❁ T (r) v) ⇒ ({u,v } ∈ E(T ))
is true for all u, v ∈ V (T ).
Suppose now that {u,v } ∈ E(T ). It is clear that u ̸=r orv ̸=r. Without loss of
generality, we assume v ̸= r. Let, as above, Pv = (v1,...,v n) ⊆ T be a path in T
withv1 =v andvn =r. Suppose that u ∈ V (Pv). Since T has no cycles, conditions
u ∈ V (Pv) and {u,v } ∈ E(Pv) imply that u =v2. As in the ﬁrst part of the proof,
we obtain v ❁ T (r) v2. Thus,
v ❁ T (r) u
PAGE 68
68 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
holds. If u /∈ V (Pv) holds, then using {u,v } ∈ E(T ) we can add the edge {u,v } to
the path Pv and obtain a path Pu = (u,v 1,...,v n) which connects u and vn =r in
T . It is clear that v ∈ Pu and, reasoning as before, we get u ❁ T (r) v. It implies
({u,v } ∈ E(T )) ⇒ (v ❁ T (r) u or u ❁ T (r) v).
Equivalence (9.29) follows. □
Remark 9.12. Let T =T (r) be a rooted tree. In the theory of trees it is often said
that a vertex u ∈ V (T ) is a direct successor of a vertex v ∈ V (T ) (= v is a direct
predecessor of u) if u ❁ T (r) v holds. By Theorem 9.9, if u ∈ V (T ) is not the root
of T , then u admits the unique direct predecessor. Moreover, Lemma 9.11 claims
that two vertices of T are adjacent if and only if one of them is a direct successor of
another.
Proposition 9.13. LetT1 =T1(r1) andT2 =T2(r2) be rooted trees and letf : V (T1) →
V (T2) be a bijective mapping. Then the following statements are eq uivalent:
(i) f is an isomorphism of rooted trees T1(r1) and T2(r2).
(ii) f is an order isomorphism of posets (V (T1), ≼T1(r1)) and (V (T2), ≼T2(r2)).
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Let f be an isomorphism of T1(r1) andT2(r2) and letu,v ∈ V (T1)
satisfy the inequality u ≼T1(r1)v. We must show that
(9.30) f (u) ≼T2(r2) f (v)
holds. By Deﬁnition 9.2, we have the equality
(9.31) f (r1) = r2.
Since r2 is the largest element of (V (T2), ≼T2(r2)), equality (9.31) implies (9.30) if
v =r1. Thus, it suﬃces to prove (9.30) if v ̸=r1. From (9.2), (9.3) and u ≼T1(r1) v
it follows that u ̸=r1 holds if v ̸=r1. Let
Pu = (u,u 1,...,u n), un =r1 and Pv = (v,v 1,...,v k), vk =r1,
be the paths (in T1) joining u with r1 and, respectively, v with r1. Then
(f (u),f (u1),...,f (un)), f(un) = r2 and (f (v),f (v1),...,f (vk)), f(vk) = r2,
are the paths (in T2) joining f (u) with r2 and, respectively, f (v) with r2. From
u ≼T1(r1)v and (9.3) we obtain the inclusion V (Pv) ⊆ V (Pu), that implies
(9.32) f (V (Pv)) ⊆ f (V (Pu)).
Now inequality (9.30) follows from (9.32) and the deﬁnition ≼T (r2). Thus, the im-
plication
(9.33) (u ≼T1(r1) v) ⇒ (f (u) ≼T2(r2)f (v))
is valid for all u, v ∈ V (T1). Since the bijection f − 1 : V (T2) → V (T1) is an iso-
morphism of the rooted trees T2(r2) and T1(r1), the converse implication for im-
plication (9.33) is also valid. Thus, f : V (T1) → V (T2) is an order isomorphism of
(V (T1), ≼T1(r1)) and (V (T2), ≼T2(r2)).
(ii) ⇒ (i). Let f : V (T1) → V (T2) be an order isomorphism of (V (T1), ≼T1(r1))
and (V (T2), ≼T2(r2)). To prove that f is an isomorphism of rooted trees T1(r1) and
T2(r2), we must show that
(9.34) f (r1) = r2
PAGE 69
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 69
holds and
(9.35) ({u,v } ∈ E(T1)) ⇔ ({f (u),f (v)} ∈ E(T2))
is valid for all u, v ∈ V (T1). Since ri is the largest element of (V (Ti), ≼Ti(ri)) and
f is an order isomorphism, equality (9.34) follows from Deﬁni tions 5.2 and 5.6. By

({u,v } ∈ E(T1)) ⇔ (v ❁ T1(r1)u or u ❁ T1(r1)v)

is valid. Now using Lemma 9.11 again, we obtain
(f (v) ❁ T2(r2)f (u) or f (u) ❁ T2(r2) f (v)) ⇔ ({f (u),f (v)} ∈ E(T2)).

9.2. Rooted representing trees of totally bounded ultrametric s paces
Let us consider now in more details the case when our tree is th e representing
tree of a totally bounded ultrametric space.
Deﬁnition 9.14. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space.

representing tree of (X,d ) and denoted as TX =TX (rX).

Theorem 9.16. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty totally bounded and ultrametric.
Then the posets (BX, ≼X) and (BY, ≼Y ) are order isomorphic if and only if the
rooted treesTX = TX (rX) and TY = TY (rY ) are isomorphic. Moreover, a bijection
Φ : BX → BY is an isomorphism of TX (rX) and TY (rY ) if and only if this bijection
is an order isomorphism of (BX, ≼X) and (BY, ≼Y ).
Proof. It follows from Lemma 9.15 and Proposition 9.13. □

It will be shown in Theorem 11.14 of the paper that the local ﬁn iteness and

representing trees of totally bounded ultrametric spaces.
10. Labelings on trees
In this section we consider the trees whose vertices are labe led by nonnegative real
numbers and, in particular, introduce the labeling on verte x sets of representing trees
of totally bounded ultrametric spaces.
The ﬁrst part of the section deals with the ultrametric space s (V (T ),d l) generated
by given labelingsl on the treesT . The structure of labeled trees T =T (l), for which
the ultrametric space (V (T ),d l) are totally bounded, is described in Theorem 10.10.
Theorem 10.9 gives us a characterization of labeled trees ha ving totally bounded
discrete (V (T ),d l).
For general totally bounded ultrametric spaces, the concep t of labeled representing
trees is introduced in Deﬁnition 10.15 of the second part of t he section. It is proved
in Theorem 10.25 that the labeled representing trees of tota lly bounded ultrametric
spaces are isomorphic if and only if the completions of these spaces are isometric.
The ultrametric space MCTX of all maximal chains of balls of a totally bounded
ultrametric space (X,d ) is investigated in the third part of the section. It is proved
in Proposition 10.29 that MCTX is isometric to the completion of (X,d ).
Labeled representing trees of p-adic balls are considered in the ﬁnal part of the
section. It is shown that such trees coincide up to isomorphi sm with irregular Bethe
Lattices of degree p + 1 labeled by numbers pk, pk− 1, pk− 2, ... with k ∈ Z.
10.1. General labeled trees
Let us introduce the concept of labeled trees.
PAGE 72
72 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Deﬁnition 10.1. A labeled tree is a pair (T,l ), where T is a tree and l is a mapping
deﬁned on the set V (T ).
As in the case of the rooted trees, we will say that T is a free tree corresponding
to (T,l ) and write T = T (l) instead of (T,l ). Moreover, in what follows, we will
consider only the nonnegative real-valued labelings l : V (T ) → R+.
For the case of labeled trees Deﬁnitions 9.1 and 9.2 must be mo diﬁed as follows.
Deﬁnition 10.2. LetTi =Ti(li) be a labeled tree, i = 1, 2. A mapping f : V (T1) →
V (T2) is an isomorphism of T1(l1) andT2(l2) if it is an isomorphism of the free trees
T1 and T2 and the equality
(10.1) l2(f (v)) = l1(v)
holds for every v ∈ V (T1).
Following [35], for arbitrary labeled tree T =T (l), we deﬁne a mapping dl : V (T )×
V (T ) → R+ as
(10.2) dl(u,v ) =
{ 0 if u =v,
max
v∗ ∈ V (P )
l(v∗) if u ̸=v,
where P is a path joining u and v in T .
Remark 10.3. The correctness of the deﬁnition of dl follows from Lemma 9.4.
To formulate the ﬁrst theorem of this section we recall the co ncept of pseudoul-
trametric space . Let X be a set. A symmetric mapping d : X × X → R+ is a
pseudoultrametric on X if
d(x,x ) = 0 and d(x,y ) ⩽ max{d(x,z ),d (z,y )}
hold for all x, y, z ∈ X. Every ultrametric is a pseudoultrametric, but a pseu-
doultrametric d on a set X is an ultrametric if and only if d(x,y )> 0 holds for all
distinct x, y ∈ X.
Theorem 10.4. Let T = T (l) be a labeled tree. Then (V (T ),d l) is a pseudoultra-
metric space. The function dl is an ultrametric on V (T ) if and only if the inequality
(10.3) max{l(u1),l (v1)}> 0
holds for every {u1,v 1} ∈ E(T ).
A proof of Theorem 10.4 can be obtained by simple modiﬁcation of the proof of
Proposition 3.2 from [36].
Proposition 10.5. LetT1 =T1(l1) andT2 =T2(l2) be labeled trees and letf : V (T1) →
V (T2) be an isomorphism of these trees. Then the equality
dl1(u,v ) = dl2(f (u),f (v))
holds for all u, v ∈ V (T1).
Proof. It directly follows from Deﬁnition 10.2 and formula (10.2), because if (v1,...,v n)
is a path joining some distinct v =v1 andu =vn inT1, then f (u) ̸=f (v) holds and
(f (v1),...,f (vn)) is a path joining f (v) and f (u) in T2 and we have the equality
{l1(v1),...,l 1(vn)} = {l2(f (v1)),...,l 2(f (vn))}. □
PAGE 73
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 73
Corollary 10.6. LetT1 =T1(l1) and T2 =T2(l2) be isomorphic labeled trees. Then
ultrametric (pseudoultrametric) spaces (V (T1),d l1) and (V (T2),d l2) are isometric.
The converse statement is not valid in general.
Example 10.7. Let V = {v0,v 1,v 2,v 3,v 4} be a ﬁve-point set, and let S =S(lS) and
P =P (lP ) be a labeled star with the center v0 and, respectively, a labeled path such
that V (S) = V (P ) = V , lS(v0) = lP (v0) = 1 and
lS(vi) + 1 = lP (vi) = 1
for i = 1, ... , 4 (see Figure 5). Then, for all distinct u, v ∈ V , we have
dlP (u,v ) = dlS (u,v ) = 1.
Thus, the ultrametric spaces (V,d lP ) and (V,d lS ) coincide, but P (lP ) and S(lS) are
not isomorphic as labeled trees or even as free trees.

Recall that a metric space (X,d ) is discrete if every point of X is isolated.
Theorem 10.9. LetT =T (l) be a labeled tree such that the inequality
max{l(u),l (v)}> 0

Vε is ﬁnite for every ε> 0.

It seems to be interesting to get a purely metric characteriz ation of totally bounded
ultrametric spaces generated by labelings on the trees. For discrete totally bounded
ultrametric spaces, such characterization, apparently, c an be given in the language
of diametrical graphs.
Remark 10.13. Theorems 10.9 and 10.10 were proved in paper [42].
Conjecture 10.1. Let (X,d ) be a discrete nonempty totally bounded ultrametric
space. Then the following statements are equivalent:
(i) There is a labeled tree T = T (l) such that (V (T ),d l) and (X,d ) are iso-
metric.
(ii) At least one of the parts of the diametrical graph GB,d|B× B is a single-point
set for every ball B ∈ BX with diamB >0.
Statement (ii) of the above conjecture can also be formulated as follows.
For every B ∈ BX, there are c ∈ X and r ⩾ 0 such that
B = {x ∈ X : d(x,c ) = r} ∪ {c}
i.e., the ball B is the sphere S(c,r ) = {x ∈ X : d(x,c ) = r} with the added
centerc.
Remark 10.14. Conjecture 10.1 was formulated in [42]. E. Petrov proved in [ 96]
the validity of Conjecture 10.1 for ﬁnite ultrametric space s using some other terms.
In [41] it is shown that the equivalence (i) ⇔ (ii) is valid for all locally ﬁnite
ultrametric spaces (X,d ).
10.2. Isomorphism of labeled representing trees
Let us deﬁne the labeling for representing trees of totally b ounded ultrametric
spaces.
Deﬁnition 10.15. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space.
The representing tree TX together with the labeling
lX : BX → R+, l X(B) = diam B,
is said to be the labeled representing tree of (X,d ) and denoted as TX =TX (lX).
Remark 10.16. Using Lemma 2.9 and Corollary 6.2, we see that, for every none mpty
totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ), the ball B = X is an unique element of
BX satisfying the equality
lX(B) = diam X.
Hence, for every totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ), the labeled representing
tree TX (lX) has a “natural” root rX = X. Consequently, for any two nonempty
totally bounded ultrametric spaces (Y,ρ ) and (Z,δ ), each isomorphism of the la-
beled representing trees TY (lY ) and TZ(lZ) is also an isomorphism of the rooted
representing trees TY (rY ) and TZ(rZ).
PAGE 78
78 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Remark 10.17. It follows directly from the deﬁnition of leaves of trees and Lemma 8.7
that v ∈ V (TX ) is a leaf of TX of and only if lX(v) = 0 holds.
Let us consider the pseudoultrametric spaces (V (TX ),d lX ) generated by the la-
beled representing trees TX (lX) of totally bounded ultrametric spaces (X,d ). To be-
gin with, it should be noted that dlX is an ultrametric on V (TX ) for every nonempty
totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ). Indeed, if {B1,B 2} ∈ E(TX ) holds, then
using Lemma 8.5 and the deﬁnition of lX we obtain
max{lX(B1),l X(B2)}> 0.
Hence, dlX is an ultrametric by Theorem 10.4.

Proposition 10.18 and Theorem 7.9 give us.
Corollary 10.19. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space with
labeled representing tree TX = TX (lX). Then the ultrametric space (V (TX ),d lX ) is

Lemma 10.20. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space with
labeled representing tree TX = TX(lX ), let B1, B2 be disjoint open balls. Then the
equality
(10.19) d(x1,x 2) = dlX (B1,B 2)
holds for every x1 ∈ B1 and every x2 ∈ B2.
Proof. Let x1 ∈ B1 and x2 ∈ B2. Then, by Proposition 2.13 and Proposition 10.18,
we have
d(x1,x 2) = diam(B1 ∪ B2) = dlX (B1,B 2). □
In what follows we write T1(l1) ≃ T2(l2) if the labeled trees T1(l1) and T2(l2) are
isomorphic.
Lemma 10.21. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be nonempty totally bounded ultrametric spaces.
If (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) are isometric, then TX(lX ) ≃ TY (lY ) holds.
Proof. Let (X,d ) and (Y,ρ ) be isometric with an isometry Φ :X → Y . Then the
equivalence
(A ∈ BX) ⇔ ({Φ(x) :x ∈ A} ∈ BY )
is valid for every A ⊆ X. Consequently, the mapping Φ∗ : BX → BY with
(10.20) Φ∗(B) = {Φ(x) :x ∈ B}, B ∈ BX,
is bijective. Proposition 8.9 implies the equalities V (TX) = BX and V (TY ) = BY .
Hence, Φ∗ is a bijective mapping from V (TX ) to V (TY ). We claim that Φ∗ is an
isomorphism of the labeled rooted trees TX(rX,l X ) and TY (rY,l Y ).
Since Φ is bijective, we have
Φ∗(rX) = Φ ∗(X) = Y =rY.
Furthermore, since Φ is an isometry, Deﬁnition 10.15 and (10.20) imply
lY (Φ(B)) = lX(B)
for every B ∈ BX = V (TX ). Now using Deﬁnition 10.2 we see that Φ∗ is an
isomorphism of the labeled rooted trees TX(rX,l X) andTY (rY,l Y ) if and only if it is
an isomorphism of free representing trees TX and TY that follows from Lemma 8.5
and (10.20), because Φ is bijective. □

Proposition 10.23. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space.
If X1 and X2 are dense subsets of X, then TX1(lX1) and TX2(lX2) are isomorphic
with an isomorphism Ψ : BX1 → BX2, where Ψ is a bijective mapping for which the
diagram

Proof. Let X1 and X2 be dense subsets of X. Using Proposition 6.12 we see that
Ψ : BX1 → BX2 is an order isomorphism of the posets (BX1, ≼X1) and (BX2, ≼X2)
and the equality
(10.35) diam Ψ(B) = diam B
holds for every B ∈ BX1. By Lemma 9.15, the equality
(BXi, ≼Xi) = (V (TXi), ≼TXi (rXi ))
holds for i = 1, 2. Hence, Ψ is also an order isomorphism of the posets
(V (TX1), ≼TX1 (rX1 )) and (V (TX2), ≼TX2 (rX2 )).
Now by Proposition 9.13, the mapping Ψ is an isomorphism of the rooted repre-
senting trees TX1(rX1) andTX2(rX2), and, consequently, it is an isomorphism of free
representing trees TX1 and TX2. Using Deﬁnition 10.15 we can rewrite (10.35) as
lX2(Ψ(B)) = lX1(B). Hence, by Deﬁnition 10.2, Ψ is an isomorphism of TX1(lX1)
and TX2(lX2). □

10.3. Maximal chains of balls and completion of space
We start by characterizing the maximal chains in the posets (V (TX), ≼TX (rX ))
generated by labeled representing tree TX =TX(lX ) (see Remark 10.16 and Propo-
sition 9.6). Recall that the presence of maximal chains is gu aranteed by Proposi-
tion 5.1 in any poset.
Lemma 10.26. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty compact ultrametric space with the rooted
representing tree TX = TX (rX). Then the following statements are equivalent for
every C ⊆ V (TX):
(i) C is a maximal chain in (V (TX ), ≼TX (rX )).
(ii) There is a point c ∈ X such that
(10.41) C = {B ∈ BX : c ∈ B}.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Suppose (i) holds. By Lemma 9.15, we have the equality
(V (TX), ≼TX (rX )) = ( BX, ≼X).
In particular, from (i) it follows that C is a maximal chain in (BX, ≼X). We must
prove that there is c ∈ X such that
(10.42) C = {B ∈ BX : B ∋ c}.
First of all, we claim that ⋂
B∈ CB is nonempty,
(10.43)
⋂
B∈ C
B ̸= ∅.
To prove that the set ⋂
B∈ CB is not empty, we will use the so-called ﬁnite inter-
section property. Recall that a nonempty family F = {Fs}s∈ S of sets has the ﬁnite
intersection property if
Fs1 ∩ Fs2 ∩ ... ∩ Fsk ̸= ∅
PAGE 84
84 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
is valid for every ﬁnite {s1,s 2,...,s k} ⊆ S. It is known that a Hausdorﬀ topological
spaceY is compact if and only if every family of closed subsets of Y , which has this
property, has nonempty intersection ([57, Theorem 3.1.1]) . Using Proposition 2.6 it
is easy to see that
k⋂
i=1
Bi ∈ BX
holds for every {B1,...,B k} ⊆ C and k ∈ N. The elements of BX are nonempty.
Thus,C has the ﬁnite intersection property that implies (10.43).
Let c be a point of ⋂
B∈ CB. By Proposition 2.13, the set
{B ∈ BX : B ∋ c}
is a chain in (BX, ≼X ). From c ∈ ⋂
B∈ CB follows the inclusion
(10.44) C ⊆ { B ∈ BX : B ∋ c}.
Since C is a maximal chain in (BX, ≼X), inclusion (10.44) yields (10.42).
(ii) ⇒ (i). Let c be a point of X for which equality (10.42) holds. Proposition 2.13
and equality (10.42) imply that C is a chain in (BX, ≼X ).
Let C 1 ∈ BX be an arbitrary chain in (BX, ≼X ) such that C 1 ⊇ C. To prove the
maximality of C it suﬃces to show that
(10.45) c ∈ B1
holds for every B1 ∈ C 1. Let B1 be an arbitrary element of C 1. By Proposition 2.5,
the ball B1 is a closed subset of X. Consequently, (10.45) holds if c is an accumu-
lation point of B1. Let ε be an arbitrary strictly positive real number. The ball
Bε(c) = {x ∈ X : d(x,c )<ε } belongs to C. Since C 1 is a chain and
B1 ∈ C 1 ⊇ C,
we have
B1 ≼X Bε(c) or Bε(c) ≼X B1.
Consequently,B1 ∩ Bε(c) ̸= ∅ holds for every ε >0. Thus, c is an accumulation
point of B1. □
Corollary 10.27. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space with

a set C ⊆ V (TX) is a maximal chain in (V (TX), ≼TX (rX )) if and only if
C = {Φ( ˜B) : ˜B ∈ ˜C}
holds for a maximal chain ˜C from (B ˜X, ≼ ˜X ). Thus, the corollary under consideration
is easily deduced from Lemma 10.26. □

10.4. Labeled representing trees of p-adic balls and spheres
In this part of the section we will consider several importan t examples of labeled
representing trees. The given below results and pictures ar e partially motivated by
papers [69] and [27].
Let p ⩾ 2 be a prime number and let Qp be the corresponding ﬁeld of all p-adic
numbers. The open ball
Br(a) = {x ∈ Qp : dp(x,a )<r }, a ∈ Qp, r> 0,
is a compact subspace of the ultrametric space (Qp,d p), where dp : Qp × Qp → R+
is the p-adic metric deﬁned as an extension of the ultrametric
⟨x,y ⟩ ↦→ | x − y|p, x,y ∈ Q
from the ﬁeld Q of rational numbers to its completion Qp (we keep the symbol dp
for p-adic metric, when passing from Q to Qp, see (3.5) and (3.6)). Consequently,
we can consider the representing tree TBr(a) of the space (Br(a),d p|Br(a)× Br (a)) as
a special example of representing trees introduced by Deﬁni tion 8.1 for all totally
bounded ultrametric spaces.
From Lemma 8.4 and Lemma 8.5 it follows that
V (TBr(a)) = {B ∈ BQp : B ⊆ Br(a)}
and, moreover, that the membership {B1,B 2} ∈ E(Br(a)) is valid if and only if we
have either
(B1 ⊂ B2) and ((B1 ⊆ B ⊆ B2) ⇒ (B1 =B or B2 =B))
or
(B2 ⊂ B1) and ((B2 ⊆ B ⊆ B1) ⇒ (B1 =B or B2 =B))
for every B ∈ V (TBr(a)). By Deﬁnition 10.15, the labeling
lBr(a) : V (TBr(a)) × V (TBr(a)) → R+
PAGE 87

satisﬁes
lBr(a)(B) = diam B = sup{dp(x,y ) :x,y ∈ B}
for every B ∈ V (TBr(a)).
Proposition 10.31. Let p ⩾ 2 be a prime number, a ∈ Qp and let r be a strictly
positive real number. Then every B ∈ V (TBr(a)) has exactly p direct successors B1,
... , Bp which are labeled such that
(10.51) lBr(a)(B1) = ... =lBr(a)(Bp) = p− 1lBr(a)(B).
Proof. Let us consider ﬁrst the case when B ∈ V (TBr(a)) coincides with Br(a). The
function
Qp ∋ x ↦→ x − a ∈ Qp
is an isometry of (Qp,d p). (This statement is well-known in the theory of p-adic
numbers or can be derived directly from formula (3.6).) Cons equently, the open ball
Br(0) = {x − a : x ∈ Br(a)}
is isometric to Br(a). By Proposition 10.22, the isometricity of Br(a) and Br(0)
implies
TBr(a)(lBr(a)) ≃ TBr(0)(lBr(0)).
Thus, without loss of generality, we can suppose a = 0.
Everyx ∈ Qp can be uniquely presented as a convergent (in (Qp,d p)) series,
(10.52) x =
∑
i∈ Z
di(x)pi,
where, for all i ∈ Z, we have di(x) ∈ { 0, 1,...,p − 1} and di = 0 holds whenever
i ⩽ i(x) with some i(x) ∈ Z. The distance set D(Qp) of the ultrametric space
(Qp,d p) has the form
(10.53) D(Qp) = {pj : j ∈ Z} ∪ { 0}.
Since (Br(0),d p|Br(0)× Br (0)) is a compact metric space and |Br(0)| ⩾ 2 holds, by
Proposition 4.11, there is x1 ∈ Br(0) such that
(10.54) diamBr(0) = dp(x1, 0)> 0.
Using (10.53) we can ﬁnd j0 =j0(x) such that
(10.55) dp(x1, 0) = pj0.
From (10.54), (10.55) and Proposition 4.11 it follows that
Br(0) = Bpj0 (0),
where Bpj0 (0) is the closed ball with the center 0 and radius pj0,
(10.56) Bpj0 (0) = {x ∈ Qp : dp(x, 0) ⩽pj0}.
Let x ∈ Qp \ {0} be given by series (10.52) and let i0 =i0(x) be deﬁned as
i0 = inf {i ∈ Z : di(x) ̸= 0}.
Then we have the equality
(10.57) dp(x, 0) = p− i0(x)
PAGE 88
88 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
(see, for example, Theorem 2.1 from Chapter II in [4]). Hence , x ∈ Qp is a point of
Bpj0 (0) if and only if x has the expansion
(10.58) x =
∑
i⩾− j0
di(x)pi.
In particular, the points x0 = 0p− j0, x1 = 1p− j0, ... , xp− 1 = (p − 1)p− j0 belong to
Bpj0 (0) and the equalities
(10.59) pj0 =dp(xk1,x k2) = diam Bpj0 (0) = diam Br(0) = diam Br(a)
hold for every pair of distinct k1, k2 ∈ { 0, 1,...,p − 1}. Using (10.57)–(10.59) we
obtain
Bpj0 (0) =
p− 1⋃
k=0
Bpj0− 1(xk)
and
Bpj0− 1(xk1) ∩ Bpj0− 1(xk2) = ∅
for every pair of distinct k1, k2 ∈ { 0, 1,...,p − 1}. From (10.53) it follows that the
equalities
(10.60) diam
Bpj0− 1(xk) = pj0− 1, Bpj0− 1(xk) = Br(xk)
hold for every r ∈ (pj0− 1,p j0] andk ∈ { 0, 1,...,p − 1}. Hence, by Theorem 4.10, the
ballsBpj0− 1(x0),... ,Bpj0− 1(xp− 1) are the parts of the complete p-partite diametrical
graph GBr(0),dp|Br (0)× Br (0).
It should be also noted that the balls Bpj0− 1(x0), ... , Bpj0− 1(xp− 1) are the direct
successors of Br(0) (see Lemma 9.11 and Remark 9.12). Now, using (10.54), (10.55 ),
(10.60) and Deﬁnition 10.15, we obtain (10.51) with B =Br(0) andBk =Bpj0− 1(xk),
k = 0, 1, ... , p − 1.
Let us consider now an arbitrary B ∈ V (TBr(a)). By Proposition 8.9, the equality
V (TBr(a)) = BBr(a)
holds. By Corollary 2.7, we have the inclusion
BB∗ ⊆ BQp
for every open ball B∗ ∈ BQp. Hence, every B ∈ V (TBr(a)) is an open ball in (Qp,d p).
Therefore, arguing as above, we can verify that every B ∈ V (TBr(a)) has exactly p
direct successors B1, ... , Bp which satisfy (10.51). □

11. From monotone labelings to ultrametric spaces and back
The main result of this section, Theorem 11.11, gives a chara cterization of the
labeled rooted trees, which are isomorphic to labeled repre senting trees of totally
bounded ultrametric spaces. This result allows us to comple tely describe the corre-
sponding free trees and rooted ones in Theorems 11.16 and 11. 14, respectively. The
characteristic properties of posets, which are order isomo rphic to balleans of totally
bounded ultrametric spaces, are presented in Theorem 11.17 .

The next concept is a generalization of the notion of monoton e rooted trees which
was proposed by V. Gurvich and M. Vyalyi in [63].
Deﬁnition 11.1. LetT =T (r) be a rooted tree and let l : V (T ) → R+ be a labeling
satisfying the conditions:
(i) The inequality
(11.1) l(v)<l (u)
holds whenever v is a direct successor of u;
(ii) The equality
(11.2) inf{l(v) :v ∈ V (C)} = 0
holds for every maximal chain C in (V (T ), ≼T (r)).
Then l is said to be a monotone labeling on T (r).
Remark 11.2. Condition (i) from the above deﬁnition means that every monotone
labelingl : V (T ) → R+ is a strictly isotone mapping of the posets (V (T ), ≼T (r)) and
(R+, ⩽) (see formula (5.1)).
Example 11.3. Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bonded ultrametric space and let
TX = TX (rX) be the rooted representing tree of (X,d ), rX = X, V (TX ) = BX.
PAGE 92
92 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
Then the labeling lX : BX → R+,
lX (B) = diam B, B ∈ BX,
is monotone on TX(rX ).
Let B1, B2 ∈ V (TX) and let B2 is a direct successor of B1. Then
B2 ❁ TX (rX ) B1
holds (see Remark 9.12). In particular, we have the proper in clusion
(11.3) B2 ⊂ B1,
that implies
diamB2 ⩽lX(B1).
If diamB1 = diamB2 holds, then from Proposition 2.10 and BX ⊆
BX (see Corol-
lary 6.2) it follows that B1 =B2, contrary (11.3). Thus, we have
lX(B2) = diam B2 < diamB1 =lX (B1).
Condition (i) of Deﬁnition 11.1 is satisﬁed.
Let C be a maximal chain in (V (TX), ≼TX (rX )) and let ( ˜X, ˜d) be a completion of
(X,d ) such that X ⊆ ˜X. By Corollary 10.27, there is c ∈ ˜X satisfying the equality
(11.4) C = { ˜B ∩ X : ˜B ∈ B ˜X and c ∈ ˜B}.
Hence, for every ε> 0 there is B ∈ C such that B = ˜Bε(c) ∩ X, where
(11.5) ˜Bε(c) = {˜x ∈ ˜X : ˜d(˜x,c )<ε }.
Now condition (ii) of Deﬁnition 11.1 follows from (11.4) and (11.5).
Proposition 11.4. Let Ti = Ti(ri) be a rooted tree with a monotone labeling li,
i = 1, 2. Then every isomorphism f : V (T1) → V (T2) of the labeled trees T1(l1) and
T2(l2) is also an isomorphism of the rooted trees T1(r1) and T2(r2).
Proof. It follows directly from Deﬁnition 11.1 that the inequality
(11.6) li(ri)>l i(vi)
holds for every vi ∈ V (Ti) \ {ri}, i = 1 , 2. Using Deﬁnition 10.2 and (11.6) we see
that if f : V (T1) → V (T2) is an isomorphism of T1(l1) and T2(l2), then the equality
(11.7) f (r1) = f (r2)
holds. Since every isomorphism of labeled trees are also an i somorphism of free
trees, Deﬁnition 9.2 and (11.7) imply that f is an isomorphism of the rooted trees
T1(r1) and T2(r2). □
The next our goal is to show that analogies of Proposition 10. 29 and Corol-
lary 10.30 remain valid for all locally ﬁnite rooted trees T = T (r) which satisfy
δ+(u) ̸= 1 for every u ∈ V (T ) and have monotone labelings.
We start with a lemma giving a “constructive” description of maximal chains in
(V (T (r)), ≼T (r)) for arbitrary rooted tree T =T (r). Below, as in Deﬁnition 9.7, the
symbolx ❁ T (r) y means that y is an upper cover of x in the poset (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)),
where ≼T (r) is deﬁned by (9.3).
Lemma 11.5. Let T = T (r) be a rooted tree, E(T ) ̸= ∅, and let C be a subset of
V (T ). Then the following statements are equivalent:
PAGE 93
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 93
(i) C is a maximal chain in (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)).
(ii) Either the elements of C can be numbered in a ﬁnite sequence c1, ... , cn
such that
(11.8) c1 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cn,
wherec1 =r and cn is a leaf of T (r) or the elements of C can be numbered
in an inﬁnite sequence (cn)n∈ N ⊆ V (T ) such that
(11.9) c1 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cn ❂ T (r) cn+1 ❂ T (r) ...
and c1 =r.
Proof. Consider ﬁrst the case when C = {c1,...,c n} is a ﬁnite maximal chain in
(V (T (r)), ≼T (r)). Condition E(T ) ̸= ∅ implies the inequality n ⩾ 2. Since C is a
chain, we can assume that
c1 ≽T (r) ... ≽T (r) cn
is satisﬁed after some renumbering, if necessary. It was sho wn in the proof of
Theorem 9.9 that r is the largest element of (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)) (see part (ii) of that
proof). Hence, we have
(11.10) r ≽T (r)c1 ≽T (r) c2 ≽T (r) ... ≽T (r)cn.
SinceC is maximal, (11.10) implies the equality r =c1. From maximality of C and
≼T (r) = ⊑ t
T (r) (see (9.15)) it follows that
ci ❂ T (r) ci+1
for every i ∈ { 1,...,n − 1}. If cn is not a leaf of T , then the inequality
(11.11) δ(cn) ⩾ 2
holds, where δ(cn) is the degree of cn in T . By Lemma 9.11, the equivalence
(11.12) (u ❂ T (r) cn or cn ❂ T (r) u) ⇔ ({cn,u } ∈ E(T ))
is valid for every u ∈ V (T ). Moreover, using Theorem 9.9 we obtain
(u ❂ T (r) cn) ⇒ (u =cn− 1)
for every u ∈ V (T ). Hence, from (11.11) and (11.12) it follows that there is a ve rtex
cn+1 ∈ V (T ) such that cn ❂ T (r) cn+1. Thus, we have the chain {c1,...,c n,c n+1},
c1 ❂ T (r) c2 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cn ❂ T (r)cn+1,
contrary to the maximality of the chain C.
Conversely, let C = {c1,...,c n} ⊆ V (T ), let (11.8) hold with c1 = r and let cn
be a leaf of T . Condition (11.8) implies that C is a chain in (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)). We
must show that C is maximal. By Lemma 9.11, (c1,...,c n) is a path joining cn
with the root r =c1 in the tree T (r). From the deﬁnition of the relation ≼T (r) (see
(9.2), (9.3)) it follows that the inequality cn ≼T (r) u holds if and only if u ∈ C.
Consequently,C is a maximal chain if cn is a minimal element of (V (T (r)), ≼T (r))
(v ≼T (r) cn impliesv =cn for every v ∈ V (T )). Since cn is a leaf of T , the inequality
(11.13) δ(cn) ⩽ 1
holds. Suppose that there is v ∈ V (T ) such that v ≺ T (r) cn. Then, by Theorem 9.9,
we can ﬁnd v∗ ∈ V (T ) such that
(11.14) v∗ ❁ T (r) cn ❁ T (r)cn− 1 ❁ T (r) ....
PAGE 94
94 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
From Lemma 9.11, (11.14) and the deﬁnition of the degree of ve rtices of graph, we
obtain the inequality δ(cn) ⩾ 2, contrary to (11.13). Hence, cn is a minimal element
of (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)). The maximality of the chain C follows.
Let us consider now the case when C is inﬁnite.
In what follows, for every x ∈ V (T ), we will denote by x∆
V (T ) and x∆
C the set
{v ∈ V (T ) :v ≽T (r) x} and, respectively, the set {c ∈ C : c ≽T (r)x}. It is clear that
(11.15) x∆
V (T ) ⊇ x∆
C
holds for every x ∈ V (T ). By Theorem 9.9, the set x∆
V (T ) is a ﬁnite chain whenever
x ∈ V (T ). If x ∈ C and C is a maximal chain, then (11.15) yields the equality
(11.16) x∆
V (T ) =x∆
C.
LetC be a maximal chain in (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)). As in the case of ﬁnite C, we obtain
r ∈ C. Write c1 =r. Since C is inﬁnite, there is c(1) ∈ C such that c1 ≻ T (r) c(1).
Using Theorem 9.9 and equality (11.16) with x = c(1), we can ﬁnd an integer
n ⩾ 2 and {c1,...,c n} ⊂ C such that
(11.17) c1 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cn
and cn = c(1). Since C is inﬁnite, from (11.17) follows the existence of an element
c(2) ∈ C for which c(1) ≻ T (r) c(2) holds. Using equality (11.16) with x =c(2) we ﬁnd
{c1,...,c n,...,c n+m} ⊂ C such that cn =c(1), cn+m =c(2) and
c1 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cn ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r)cn+m.
Repeating this procedure we obtain a sequence (cn)n∈ N ⊆ C such that ci ❂ T (r) ci+1
holds for every i ∈ N. We claim that the equality
(11.18) C = {cn : n ∈ N}
holds. Since (cn)n∈ N is a sequence of elements of C, equality (11.18) holds if we have
(11.19) c ∈ {cn : n ∈ N}
for every c ∈ C.
Let c be an arbitrary element of C. Suppose there is p ∈ {cn : n ∈ N} such that
p ≼T (r) c. Then we obtain
c ∈ p∆
V (T ) ⊆ { cn : n ∈ N},
i.e., (11.19) holds. Since C is a chain, we have c ≼T (r) cn orcn ≼T (r) c for all n ∈ N.
Consequently, if (11.19) does not hold, then c ≼T (r) cn is valid for every n ∈ N. It
implies the inclusion
(11.20) {cn : n ∈ N} ⊆ c∆
V (T ).
The set c∆
V (T ) is ﬁnite by Theorem 9.9. Hence, from (11.20) and Theorem 9.9 i t
follows that {cn : n ∈ N} is also ﬁnite, contrary to the construction.
A minor modiﬁcation of the last part of the above proof shows t hat any chainC ad-
mitting a numbering such that (11.9) holds withc1 =r is maximal in (V (T (r)), ≼T (r)).
The proof is completed. □
Corollary 11.6. LetT =T (r) be a rooted tree with a monotone labeling l : V (T ) →
R+ and let v ∈ V (T ). Then l(v) = 0 holds if and only if v is a leaf of T .

Proof. It follows directly from Deﬁnition 11.1 and the one-to-one c orrespondence
between the leaves and ﬁnite maximal chains described in Lem ma 11.5. We recall
only that the root r is a leaf of T when T is an empty tree (see Remark 9.17). □
Lemma 11.7. LetT =T (r) be a rooted tree, let C1, C2 be diﬀerent maximal chains
in (V (T ), ≼T (r)). Then we have
(11.21) c1 ∥T (r)c2
for all c1 ∈ C1 \(C1 ∩ C2) and c2 ∈ C2 \(C1 ∩ C2).
Proof. Suppose that there exist c1 ∈ C1 \(C1 ∩C2) andc2 ∈ C2 \(C1 ∩C2) for which
(11.21) does not hold. Then, without loss of generality, we c an assume c1 ≺ T (r) c2.
From Theorem 9.9 and the deﬁnition of the partial order ≼T (r) it follows that c2 is
an vertex of the path Pc1 = (v1,...,v n) joining c1 with r in T (r) and the relations
(11.22) c1 =v1 ❁ T (r) v2 ❁ T (r) ... ❁ T (r) vn =r
hold. Lemma 9.4 (the uniqueness of a path connected c1 and r in T (r)) and
Lemma 11.5 imply V (Pc1) ⊆ C1. Consequently, c2 ∈ C1 holds, that yields c2 ∈
C1 ∩ C2, contrary to c2 ∈ C2 \(C1 ∩ C2). □

Proposition 11.8. For every rooted treeT =T (r) with a monotone labeling l : V (T ) →
R+ the mapping
dMCT (r) : MCT (r) × MCT (r) → R+
is an ultrametric on MCT (r). In addition, if T is locally ﬁnite, then the ultrametric
space (MCT (r),d MCT (r)) is compact.
Proof. LetT =T (r) be a rooted tree and let l : V (T ) → R+ be a monotone labeling.
First of all, we want to prove that dMCT (r) is an ultrametric.
It is easy to prove that the inequality dMCT (r)(C1,C 2) > 0 holds for all distinct
C1, C2 ∈ MCT (r). Indeed, if C1 ̸= C2, then, as in Remark 10.28, we obtain that
there is v∗ ∈ C1 ∩ C2 such that v∗ = ∧ (C1 ∩ C2). From C1 ̸= C2, Lemma 11.5
and Corollary 11.6 it follows that v∗ is not a leaf of T . Consequently, dMCT (r) is an
ultrametric if |MCT (r)|⩽ 2.
Let C1, C2, C3 be pairwise diﬀerent maximal chains in (V (T ), ≼T (r)). To prove
the strong triangle inequality
(11.24) dMCT (r)(C1,C 2) ⩽ max{dMCT (r)(C1,C 3),d MCT (r)(C2,C 3)},
we note that the set {∧ (C1 ∩C2), ∧ (C2 ∩C3), ∧ (C3 ∩C1)} is a chain in (V (T ), ≼T (r)).
Really, if i, j, k ∈ { 1, 2, 3} and i ̸=j ̸=k, then
(11.25) ∧ (Ci ∩ Cj) ∈ Cj and ∧ (Cj ∩ Ck) ∈ Cj
hold. Since Cj is a chain in (V (T ), ≼T (r)), from (11.25) it follows that
∧ (Ci ∩ Cj) ≼T (r) ∧ (Cj ∩ Ck) or ∧ (Cj ∩ Ck) ≼T (r) ∧ (Ci ∩ Cj).
It is easy to see that inequality (11.24) is valid if
(11.26) ∧ (C1 ∩ C3) ∈ C1 ∩ C2 or ∧ (C3 ∩ C2) ∈ C1 ∩ C2.
If (11.26) does not hold, then we obtain
(11.27) ∧ (C1 ∩ C3) ∈ C1 \(C1 ∩ C2) and ∧ (C3 ∩ C2) ∈ C2 \(C1 ∩ C2).
By Lemma 11.7, from (11.27) follows
∧ (C1 ∩ C3) ∥T (r) ∧ (C3 ∩ C2).
Hence, {∧ (C1 ∩C2), ∧ (C2 ∩C3), ∧ (C3 ∩C1)} is not a chain in (V (T ), ≼T (r)), contrary
to the statement which was obtained in the ﬁrst part of the pro of.
Thus, (MCT (r),d MCT (r)) is an ultrametric space.
Let T be locally ﬁnite. By Bolzano—Weierstrass property (see Pro position 3.2),
the metric space (MCT (r),d MCT (r)) is compact if and only if, for every sequence
(C n)n∈ N ⊆ MCT (r), there are C ∈ MCT (r) and a subsequence (C nj )j∈ N of the
sequence (C n)n∈ N such that
(11.28) lim
j→∞
dMCT (r)(C nj,C ) = 0.
Let (C n)n∈ N ⊆ MCT (r). We will use an induction to construct the desirable (C nj )j∈ N
and C. It suﬃces to consider the case when all elements of (C n)n∈ N are pairwise
diﬀerent,
(11.29) (C n ̸=C m) ⇔ (n ̸=m) ∀n,m ∈ N.
Write A1 = {C n : n ∈ N}, n1 = 1, and c1 =r. From (11.29) it follows |V (T )|= ∞ .
Consequently, the set of all direct successors of r is nonempty. In addition, this set
PAGE 97
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 97
is ﬁnite, because T is locally ﬁnite. Using Lemma 11.5 and (11.29), we can ﬁnd
c2 ∈ V (T ) such that c1 ❂ T (r) c2 holds and the set
A2 = {C n ∈ A1 : {c1,c 2} ⊆ C n,n ∈ N}
is inﬁnite. The set of all direct successors of c2 is also ﬁnite and nonempty. Hence,
there is c3 ∈ V (T ) such that c1 ❂ T (r) c2 ❂ T (r) c3 and the set
A3 = {C n ∈ A2 : {c1,c 2,c 3} ⊆ C n,n ∈ N}
is inﬁnite. Repeating this procedure, we can ﬁnd a sequence (cj)j∈ N ⊆ V (T ) and a
sequence (Aj)j∈ N ⊆ 2V (T ) such that
(11.30) c1 ❂ T (r) ... ❂ T (r) cj ❂ T (r) cj+1 ❂ T (r) ...,
c1 =r and
(11.31) {c1,...,c j} ⊆ Aj ⊆ { C n : n ∈ N}
for every j ∈ N. Write C = {cj : j ∈ N}. By Lemma 11.5, (11.30) implies that C
is a maximal chain in (V (T ), ≼T (r)). Moreover, from (11.31) and (11.23) it follows
that
(11.32) dMCT (r)(C,A ) ⩽l(cj)
holds for every j ∈ N and every A ∈ Aj. The sequence (Aj)j∈ N ⊆ 2V (T ), satisfying
Aj ∈ Aj for every j ∈ N, is a subsequence of the sequence (C n)n∈ N ⊆ MCT (r). Now
condition (i) of Deﬁnition 11.1 and inequality (11.32) give us limit relat ion (11.28)
with C nj =Aj. □
The next lemma describes the structure of open balls in the sp ace (MCT (r),d MCT (r)).

Proposition 11.10. LetT =T (r) be a locally ﬁnite rooted tree and let l : V (T ) →
R+ be a monotone labeling on T (r). Then the following statements are equivalent:
(i) The labeled representing tree TMCT (r)(lMCT (r)) of the compact ultrametric
space (MCT (r),d MCT (r)) is isomorphic to the labeled tree T (l).
(ii) For every u ∈ V (T ) we have δ+
T (r)(u) ̸= 1.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Let (i) hold. Then there is an isomorphism
Φ :V (TMCT (r)) → V (T )
of the labeled representing tree TMCT (r)(lMCT (r)) and the labeled tree T (l). The
labelings
l : V (T ) → R+ and lMCT (r) : V (TMCT (r)) → R+
PAGE 102
102 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
are monotone (see Example 11.3). Hence, by Proposition 11.4 , Φ is an isomorphism
of the rooted trees TMCT (r)(rMCT (r)) andT (r). Now statement (ii) of the proposition
being proved follows from Proposition 9.18.
(ii) ⇒ (i). Let (ii) hold. Then, by Lemma 11.9, the mapping
Φ :V (T ) → BMCT (r)
with
(11.66) Φ(v) = {C ∈ MCT (r) : v ∈ C}
is correctly deﬁned and surjective. By Proposition 11.8, th e space (MCT (r),d MCT (r))
is ultrametric and compact and, consequently, by Lemma 8.4, the equality
V (TMCT (r)) = BMCT (r)
holds. Hence, it suﬃces to show that Φ is an isomorphism of the labeled trees T (l)
and TMCT (r)(lMCT (r)).
Analyzing of the proof of Lemma 11.9, we obtain the equality
(11.67) diam Φ(v) = l(v)
for everyv ∈ V (T ) (see, in particular, (11.52), when v is a leaf of T and, respectively,
(11.56), whenv is not a leaf of T ). Hence, it suﬃces to show that Φ is an isomorphism
of T (r) and TMCT (r)(rMCT (r)).
Let us show ﬁrst that the mapping Φ is injective. Suppose contrary that there
existv1, v2 ∈ V (T ) such that
(11.68) v1 ̸=v2 and Φ(v1) = Φ(v2).
Let C1 and C2 belong to MCT (r) such that v1 ∈ C1 and v2 ∈ C2. From (11.67)
and (11.68) it follows that l(v1) = l(v2) holds. The last equality, Lemma 11.5,
Remark 11.2 and v1 ̸= v2 imply v1 ∥T (r) v2. In particular, we obtain C1 ̸= C2.
Consequently, the equality
dMCT (r)(C1,C 2) = l(∧ (C1 ∩ C2))
holds (see (11.23)). From v1 ∥T (r) v2 it follows that vi /∈ C1 ∩ C2, i = 1 , 2. Hence,
we have
vi ≺ T (r) ∧ (C1 ∩ C2), i = 1, 2,
that implies the inequality
(11.69) l(vi)<d MCT (r)(C1,C 2)
(see Remark 11.2). Using (11.67) we see that l(v1)(= l(v2)) is the diameter of the
ball Φ(v1)(= Φ(v2)) which contradicts (11.69) because C1 andC2 belong to this ball.
Thus, we proved that Φ is an injective mapping.
It was noted above that Φ is also surjective. Hence, Φ :V (T ) → BMCT (r) is a
bijection. By Proposition 9.13, the bijection Φ is an isomorphism of the rooted
tree T (r) and TMCT (r)(rMCT (r)) if nd only if it is an order isomorphism of posets
(V (T ), ≼T (r)) and (V (TMCT (r)), ≼TMCT (r)
). Lemma 9.15 implies the equality
(V (TMCT (r)), ≼TMCT (r)
) = ( BMCT (r), ≼MCT (r)).
Thus, it suﬃces to prove that Φ is an order isomorphism of the posets
(V (T ), ≼T (r)) and (BMCT (r), ≼MCT (r)).
PAGE 103
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 103
Using Lemma 11.5 and the uniqueness of upper cover in (V (T ), ≼T (r)) we can simply
prove the validity of the implication
(u ❁ T (r) v) ⇒ (Φ(u) ≼MCT (r) Φ(v))
for all u, v ∈ V (T ) that, together with Theorem 9.9, proves the validity of
(p ≼T (r) q) ⇒ (Φ(p) ≼MCT (r) Φ(q))
for all p, q ∈ V (T ). Thus, Φ is isotone as a mapping from (V (T ), ≼T (r)) to
(BMCT (r), ≼MCT (r)). Since Φ is bijective, the last statement implies that Φ is strictly
isotone (see formula (5.1)). Hence, by Lemma 5.3, it suﬃces t o prove the validity
of implication
(11.70) (v1 ∥T (r)v2) ⇒ (Φ(v1) ∥MCT (r) Φ(v2))
for all v1, v2 ∈ V (T ).
Let v1, v2 ∈ V (T ) and v1 ∥T (r) v2 hold. Suppose that Φ(v1) ∥MCT (r) Φ(v2) is
false. Using Proposition 2.13 we see that Φ(v1) ∥MCT (r) Φ(v2) is false if and only if
Φ(v1) ∩ Φ(v2) ̸= ∅. Without loss of generality, we may assume that l(v2) ⩽ l(v1).
Then the equalities diam Φ(v1) = l(v1), diam Φ(v2) = l(v2), the inclusion
BMCT (r) ⊆
BMCT (r)
(see Corollary 6.2) and Proposition 2.10 imply
(11.71) Φ(v2) ⊆ Φ(v1).
As in the proof of injectivity of Φ, we can show that
(11.72) diam Φ(v1) = l(v1)<d MCT (r)(C1,C 2)
holds whenever Ci ∈ MCT (r) and vi ∈ Ci, i = 1, 2. By (11.66), we have C1 ∈ Φ(v1)
andC2 ∈ Φ(v2). Hence, (11.72) contradicts (11.71). Implication (11.70) is valid. □
The next theorem can be considered as a main result of the pape r.
Theorem 11.11. LetT =T (l) be a labeled tree. Then following statements (i)–(iii)
are equivalent:
(i) There is a nonempty compact ultrametric space (X,d ) such that TX(lX) ≃
T (l).
(ii) There is a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (Y,ρ ) such that
TY (lY ) ≃ T (l).
(iii) T is locally ﬁnite and there is r ∈ V (T ) such that l is a monotone labeling
on T (r) and for every u ∈ V (T ) we have
(11.73) δ+
T (r)(u) ̸= 1.
Proof. By Theorem 10.25, the logical equivalence (i) ⇔ (ii) is valid.
(i) ⇒ (iii). Suppose that there is a nonempty compact ultrametric space (X,d )
such that TX(lX ) ≃ T (l). Then T is locally ﬁnite by Proposition 8.9. Let
Φ :V (TX ) → V (T )
be an isomorphism of labeled trees TX (lX) andT (l). The labeling lX : V (TX) → R+
is monotone on TX(rX) (see Example 11.3). Consequently, l is a monotone labeling
on the rooted tree T = T (r) with r = Φ(X). Hence, by Proposition 11.4, Φ is
PAGE 104
104 OLEKSIY DOVGOSHEY
also an isomorphism of the tooted trees TX (rX) and T (r), r = Φ(X). Now (11.73)
follows from formula (9.45).
(iii) ⇒ (i). Suppose (iii) holds. Let us denote by r the vertex of T for which l is
monotone on T (r) and (11.73) holds for every u ∈ V (T ). Since T is locally ﬁnite,
the set MCT (r) endowed with metric dMCT (r) (see (11.23)) is a compact ultrametric
space by Proposition 11.8. In addition, since we have (11.73 ) for every u ∈ V (T ),
Proposition 11.10 implies
T (l) ≃ TMCT (r)(lMCT (r)).
Statement (i) follows. □
Theorem 11.11 guarantees, in particular, the existence of G urvich—Vyalyi repre-
sentation of ﬁnite ultrametric spaces by monotone trees.
Corollary 11.12. Let T = T (l) be a ﬁnite labeled tree. Then the following state-
ments (i) and (ii) are equivalent.
(i) There is a nonempty ﬁnite ultrametric space (X,d ) with TX (lX) ≃ T (l).
(ii) There is r ∈ V (T ) such that:
(ii1) δ+
T (r)(u) ̸= 1 for every u ∈ V (T );
(ii2) l(v)<l (u) whenever v is a direct successor of u;
(ii3) l(u) = 0 if and only if u is a leaf of T (r).
Remark 11.13. IfT =T (l) is a ﬁnite labeled tree satisfying statement (ii2) of Corol-
lary 11.12, then T (l) ≃ TX (lX) is valid for unique up to isometry ﬁnite ultrametric
space (X,d ) (see Theorem 2.7 [49] and Theorem 3.6 [33]).
Using Theorem 11.11 we can simply obtain a characterization of the rooted rep-
resenting trees of totally bounded ultrametric spaces.

labeling l : V (T ) → R+ on T (r).

Now we can completely describe the free representing trees o f totally bounded
ultrametric spaces. Let us start from the next example.
Example 11.15. Let T be a tree with |V (T )|= 2. Then there is no totally bounded
ultrametric space (X,d ) such that the trees T and TX are isomorphic. Indeed, if
(X,d ) is a totally bounded ultrametric space for which |V (TX)| = 2 , then we have
|X|⩾ 2. By Proposition 4.11, the diametrical graph GX,d is complete k-partite for
an integer k ⩾ 2. Thus, the inequality |V (TX )|⩾ 3 holds whenever |X|⩾ 2.
Theorem 11.16. The following conditions are equivalent for every tree T :
(i) There is a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ) such that the
free representing tree TX and T are isomorphic.
(ii) T is a locally ﬁnite tree with |V (T )| ̸= 2 and the equation δ(v) = 2 has at
most one solution v ∈ V (T ).
Proof. Proposition 8.9 and Example 11.15 imply the validity of (i) ⇒ (ii).

δT (v) = 2 has at most one solution v ∈ V (T ). Since any two isomorphic rooted

The next theorem is a characterization of ordered balleans (BX, ≼X ) of totally
bounded ultrametric spaces (X,d ) up to order isomorphisms.
Theorem 11.17. Let (S, ≼S) be a poset. Then conditions (i) and (ii) are equivalent.
(i) There is a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ) such that the
posets (BX, ≼X ) and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic.
(ii) The poset (S, ≼S) has the following properties:
(ii1) S contains a largest element l.
(ii2) If p ∈ S is not the largest element of S, then there is a unique
q ∈ S such that p ❁ S q.
(ii3) If b ∈ S is not a minimal element of S, then the number of lower
covers of b is ﬁnite and greater than or equal to two, i.e., there are
distinct elements p1, ... , pn ∈ S such that n ⩾ 2 and
(p ❁ S b) ⇔ (p ∈ {p1,...,p n})
is valid for every p ∈ S.
(ii4) The equality ≼S = ⊑ t
S holds.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii). Let (X,d ) be a nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space
for which (BX, ≼X ) and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic. We must show that (S, ≼S) has
properties (ii1)– (ii4).
By Lemma 9.15, we have the equality
(11.78) (BX, ≼X) = (V (TX (rX)), ≼TX (rX )).
Consequently, the posets (V (TX (rX)), ≼TX (rX )) and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic. By The-
orem 9.9 it implies that (S, ≼S) has properties (ii1), (ii2) and (ii4). Since (BX, ≼X)
and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic, (11.78) implies that (S, ≼S) has property (ii3) if and
only if the following statement holds for every B∗ ∈ BX with |B∗|⩾ 2:
(s1) There are an integer number n ⩾ 2 and a set {B1,...,B n} ⊆ BX such that
(B ❁ TX (rX )B∗) ⇔ (B ∈ {B1,...,B n}), ∀B ∈ BX,
where ❁ TX (rX ) the upper covering relation corresponding to the partial or der
≼TX (rX ).
From Lemma 9.11 and the deﬁnition of out-degree of vertices o f rooted trees it
follows that statement (s1) is true if and only if
(11.79) +∞ >δ +(B∗) ⩾ 2
holds for every B∗ ∈ BX with |B∗| ⩾ 2. To complete the veriﬁcation of (ii3) it
suﬃces to note that (11.79) follows from Proposition 9.18 an d equality (9.45).
(ii) ⇒ (i). Let (S, ≼S) have properties (ii1)– (ii4). We want to prove the existence
of nonempty totally bounded ultrametric space (X,d ) for which the posets (BX, ≼X)
and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic.
First of all, we note that condition (ii) of Theorem 11.17 implies condition (ii)
of Theorem 9.9. Hence, there is a rooted tree T ∗(r∗) such that (V (T ∗(r∗)), ≼T ∗ (r∗ ))
and (S, ≼S) are order isomorphic. Suppose also that

Let F : V (T ∗(r∗)) → V (TX (rX)) be an isomorphism of these rooted trees and let
Φ :S → V (T ∗(r∗)) be an order isomorphism of (S, ≼S) and (V (T ∗(r∗)), ≼T ∗ (r∗ )). By

holds for every u∗ ∈ V (T ∗(r∗)). Using Lemma 9.11 we obtain

The following example shows that properties (ii1)– (ii4), which characterized the
ordering on balleans of totally bounded ultrametric spaces , are logically independent.
Example 11.18. (e1) Let (X,d ) = ( Q2,d 2) be the ﬁeld of 2-adic numbers with the

and, consequently, (BX, ≼X ) does not contain any largest element. The validity of
(ii2) & (ii3) & (ii4) follows from Theorem 11.17 and the formula
D(Q2) = {2i : i ∈ Z} ∪ { 0}
that described the distance set of (Q2,d 2) (see (3.5)). (Note that every B ∈ BX is
a compact ultrametric space.)

For ﬁnite posets, Theorem 11.17 implies the following.
Corollary 11.19. Let (S, ≼S) be a nonempty ﬁnite poset. Then conditions (i) and
(ii) are equivalent.
(i) There is a nonempty ﬁnite ultrametric space (X,d ) such that the posets
(BX, ≼X) and (S, ≼S) are isomorphic.
(ii) The poset (S, ≼S) has the following properties:
(ii1) If p ∈ S is not the largest element of S, then there is a unique
q ∈ S such that p ❁ S q.
(ii2) If b ∈ S is not a minimal element of S, then the number of lower
covers of b is greater than or equal to two.
Proof. The validity of implication (i) ⇒ (ii) directly follows from Theorem 11.17.
To prove the truth of implication (ii) ⇒ (i) we note that the equality ≼S = ⊑ t
S
holds because S is ﬁnite. Moreover, the ﬁniteness of S and condition (ii1) imply
the existence of the largest element in S. Indeed, since S is nonempty and ﬁnite,
S contains a maximal element which is the largest element of S by property (ii1).
Consequently, condition (ii) of Theorem 11.17 follows from condition (ii) of the
present corollary if S is ﬁnite. Now using Theorem 11.17 we obtain the existence of
desirable ﬁnite (X,d ). □
Remark 11.20. It was noted in [51] that, for every non-ultrametric, three- point met-
ric space (X,d ), the poset (BX, ≼X ) is order isomorphic to the poset (S, ≼S) depicted
by Figure 14. If a tree-point metric space (X,d ) is ultrametric, then (BX, ≼X) is
order isomorphic either to the poset (S1, ≼S1) or to the poset (S2, ≼S2) depicted in
PAGE 109
TOTALLY BOUNDED ULTRAMETRIC SPACES AND LOCALLY FINITE TREE S 109
Figure 15. Thus, Figures 14 and 15 give us all posets which are order isomorphic to
(BX, ≼X) with |X|= 3.
(S1, ≼S1)l1 (S2, ≼S2)l2
Figure 15. If (X,d ) is ultrametric and |X| = |D(X)| = 3 holds,
then (BX, ≼X ) is isomorphic to (S1, ≼S1). For ultrametric space (X,d )
satisfying |X|= |D(X)|+ 1 = 3 , the poset (BX, ≼X) is isomorphic to
(S2, ≼S2).
The above remark leads to the following.
Problem 11.21. Using the theory of directed graphs describe up to isomorphi sm
the ordered balleans of all ﬁnite metric spaces.
For the case of ﬁnite, ultrametric spaces Problem 11.21 is cl osely connected with
the problem of I. M. Gelfand [85] that is the starting point of the present paper. It
should be noted here that Problem 11.21 is also related to the problem of combina-
torial classiﬁcation of ﬁnite metric spaces via their funda mental polytopes that was
proposed by A. M. Vershik [128] (see also [29] for some result s in this direction).
Remark 11.22. The locally ﬁnite, inﬁnite trees have been intensively stud ied by
many mathematicians of the last several decades. One of the m ost interesting areas
of such research is connected with the so-called “Reconstru ction Conjecture”. This
conjecture claims that two ﬁnite graphs G andH with |V (G)|= |V (H)|⩾ 3 are iso-
morphic ifG andH are hypomorphic, i.e., there is a bijection g : V (G) → V (H) such
that the induced subgraphs G − v andH − g(v) are isomorphic for every v ∈ V (G).
Kelly [79] proved the Reconstruction Conjecture for ﬁnite t rees. An survey of results
connected with this conjecture was given by Bondy and Hemmin ger [15]. A variant
of the Reconstruction Conjecture for locally ﬁnite trees wa s proposed by Harary,
Schwenk and Scott [64] but Bowler, Erde, Heinig Lehner and Pi tz [21] found hypo-
morphic, locally ﬁnite trees which are not isomorphic. The r econstruction of rooted
trees was considered T. Andreae [3]. It seams to be interesti ng to translate the
theorem Bowler—Erde—Heinig—Lehner—Pitz and some results related to Recon-
struction Conjecture (see, for example, [2, 3]) on the langu age of ultrametric spaces.
